<p><b>Joseph Jacques Jean Chrétien</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit" class="mw-redirect" title="Member of the Order of Merit">OM</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/King%27s_Counsel" title="King&#39;s Counsel">KC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Bar_of_Quebec" title="Bar of Quebec">AdE</a></span> (<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1177148991">.mw-parser-output .IPA-label-small{font-size:85%}.mw-parser-output .references .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .infobox .IPA-label-small,.mw-parser-output .navbox .IPA-label-small{font-size:100%}</style><span class="IPA-label IPA-label-small">French pronunciation:</span> <span class="IPA nowrap" lang="fr-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/French" title="Help:IPA/French">&#91;ʒɑ̃<span class="wrap"> </span>kʁe.tsjɛ̃&#93;</a></span>; born January 11, 1934) is a Canadian lawyer and politician who served as the 20th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1993 to 2003.
</p><p>Born and raised in <a href="/wiki/Shawinigan" title="Shawinigan">Shawinigan Falls</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, Chrétien is a law graduate from <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Université Laval</a>. A <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a>, he was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons</a> in <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">1963</a>. He served in various cabinet posts under Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>, most prominently as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Indian_Affairs_and_Northern_Development" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Indian Affairs and Northern Development">minister of Indian affairs and northern development</a>, <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Treasury_Board" title="President of the Treasury Board">president of the Treasury Board</a>, <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">minister of finance</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">minister of justice</a>. He ran unsuccessfully for the leadership of the Liberal Party in <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1984</a>, losing to <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>. Chrétien served as <a href="/wiki/Deputy_prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Deputy prime minister of Canada">deputy prime minister</a> in Turner's short-lived government which would be defeated in the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 federal election</a>. After Turner led the Liberals to their <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">second defeat at the polls in 1988</a>, Chrétien became leader of the Liberals and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Leader of the Official Opposition (Canada)">leader of the Opposition</a> in <a href="/wiki/1990_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1990 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1990</a>, returning to politics after briefly working in the private sector. In the <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">1993 federal election</a>, Chrétien led the Liberals to a strong <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a> before leading the party to two additional majorities in <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">1997</a> and <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">2000</a>.
</p><p>Chrétien was strongly opposed to the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement" title="Quebec sovereignty movement">Quebec sovereignty movement</a>. He won a narrow victory as leader of the federalist camp in the <a href="/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum" title="1995 Quebec referendum">1995 Quebec referendum</a>, and then pioneered the <i><a href="/wiki/Clarity_Act" title="Clarity Act">Clarity Act</a></i> to avoid ambiguity in future referendum questions. <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Premiership of Jean Chrétien">His government</a> also established the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Firearms_Registry" title="Canadian Firearms Registry">long-gun registry</a>, advanced the <i><a href="/wiki/Youth_Criminal_Justice_Act" title="Youth Criminal Justice Act">Youth Criminal Justice Act</a></i>, laid the groundwork to legalize <a href="/wiki/Same-sex_marriage_in_Canada" title="Same-sex marriage in Canada">same-sex marriage</a>, and <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1997 Canadian federal budget">eliminated</a> the nearly-30-year old budget <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending" title="Deficit spending">deficit</a> mainly through spending cuts. He implemented several major environmental laws, including an updated <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Protection_Act,_1999" title="Canadian Environmental Protection Act, 1999"><i>Canadian Environmental Protection Act</i></a>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Pest_Control_Products_Act" title="Pest Control Products Act">Pest Control Products Act</a></i>, and the <i><a href="/wiki/Species_at_Risk_Act" title="Species at Risk Act">Species At Risk Act</a></i>. In foreign policy, Chrétien ordered Canadian military intervention during the <a href="/wiki/NATO_bombing_of_Yugoslavia" title="NATO bombing of Yugoslavia">NATO bombing of Yugoslavia</a> and the <a href="/wiki/War_in_Afghanistan_(2001%E2%80%932021)" title="War in Afghanistan (2001–2021)">War in Afghanistan</a> but opposed participation in the <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">Iraq War</a>. Although his popularity and that of the Liberal Party were seemingly unchallenged for three consecutive federal elections, he became subject to various political controversies. He was accused of inappropriate behaviour in the <a href="/wiki/Shawinigate" title="Shawinigate">Shawinigate</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sponsorship_scandal" title="Sponsorship scandal">sponsorship</a> scandals, although he has consistently denied any wrongdoing. He also became embroiled in <a href="#Chrétien_and_Martin:_Liberal_Party_infighting">a protracted leadership struggle</a> within the Liberal Party against his <a href="/wiki/Finance_Minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Finance Minister of Canada">finance minister</a> and long-time political rival <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a>. In December 2003, as a result of the threat of losing a <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a> and pressure from the pro-Martin faction of the party, Chrétien resigned as prime minister and retired from politics. Chrétien ranks above-average in <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_Canadian_prime_ministers" class="mw-redirect" title="Historical rankings of Canadian prime ministers">rankings of Canadian prime ministers</a>. At age <span class="currentage"></span>90, Chrétien is the oldest living former Canadian prime minister.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life.2C_family.2C_and_education"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life,_family,_and_education">Early life, family, and education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life, family, and education"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chrétien was born on January 11, 1934, in <a href="/wiki/Shawinigan" title="Shawinigan">Shawinigan Falls</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, as the 18th of 19 children (10 of whom did not survive infancy),<sup id="cite_ref-L&amp;T_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-L&amp;T-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> of Marie (née Boisvert, 1892–1954) and Wellie Chrétien (1887–1980).<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> His younger brother is the <a href="/wiki/Neuroendocrinology" title="Neuroendocrinology">neuroendocrinology</a> researcher <a href="/wiki/Michel_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Michel Chrétien">Michel Chrétien</a>. The working-class Chrétien family was poor, and Chrétien had to wear clothing that had been worn by his siblings as his parents were too indigent to buy new clothing for him.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._27_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._27-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's parents wanted their children to escape a working-class life in Shawinigan by attending a <a href="/wiki/Classical_college" title="Classical college">classical college</a>, which was the only way one could attend university in Quebec at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._27_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._27-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's father made him read the dictionary as a young boy. Chrétien's older brother Maurice won a scholarship at the insurance company he was working for, which allowed him to attend medical school, and with the profits from his medical practice, was able to assist his younger siblings to attend the classical colleges.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Wellie Chrétien was a staunch Liberal who once got to shake hands as a young man with his hero, <a href="/wiki/Wilfrid_Laurier" title="Wilfrid Laurier">Sir Wilfrid Laurier</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._44_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._44-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> The local parish priest, Father Auger, a supporter of the <a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a> who hated all Liberals as "ungodly," spread malicious rumours about the Liberal Chrétien family, saying he would never let a teenage girl go on a date unchaperoned with any of the Chrétien boys, which caused the young Jean Chrétien to have troubled relations with the Catholic church.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._89_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._89-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, the Canadian nationalist Wellie Chrétien had attracted much public disapproval by being a staunch supporter of the war effort, and especially by being one of the few French-Canadians in Shawinigan willing to publicly support sending the conscripts (known as "Zombies") to fight overseas.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> Under the 1940 <i><a href="/wiki/National_Resources_Mobilization_Act" title="National Resources Mobilization Act">National Resources Mobilization Act</a></i>, the federal government could conscript Canadians only for the defence of Canada, and until late 1944, only volunteers went to fight overseas. In 1940s Quebec, where many French-Canadians were opposed to Canada fighting in the war, and especially to sending the "Zombies" overseas, this made Wellie Chrétien and his family outcasts.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._44_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._44-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Furthermore, during the <a href="/wiki/Grande_Noirceur" title="Grande Noirceur">Grande Noirceur</a> ("<i>Great Darkness</i>") when Quebec society was dominated by the corrupt Union Nationale patronage machine, the Chrétien family were excluded because of Wellie Chrétien's support for the war.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The Union Nationale Premier <a href="/wiki/Maurice_Duplessis" title="Maurice Duplessis">Maurice Duplessis</a> had been an outspoken opponent of Canadian participation in World War II. Until 1964, Quebec had no public schools, and Chrétien was educated in Catholic schools. Chrétien disliked the Catholic priests who educated him and in turn was disliked by them with one of Chrétien's former teachers, Father François Lanoue, recalling that Chrétien was the only student he ever grabbed by his ears, as he was too unruly.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> In an interview, Chrétien called his education "unnatural", as he recalled an extremely strict regime where the priests beat anyone bloody who dared to question their authority while teaching via rote learning.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceD_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceD-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> One of Chrétien's classmates recalled "We didn't have the right to have feelings or express them".<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceD_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceD-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien got his early schooling at a private boys' school in <a href="/wiki/Joliette" title="Joliette">Joliette</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> He then attended <a href="/wiki/S%C3%A9minaire_Saint-Joseph_de_Trois-Rivi%C3%A8res" title="Séminaire Saint-Joseph de Trois-Rivières">Séminaire Saint-Joseph de Trois-Rivières</a>. He obtained excellent grades and then studied law at <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Université Laval</a>, the training ground of the French-Canadian elite.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> As a student at Trois-Rivières, Chrétien later recalled that his best day at that school was his first day when he attacked without provocation another student taller than himself, leading him to proudly remember that: "I really socked it to him bad. In front of everybody!"<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._51_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._51-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien recalled that his assault was meant to send the message to the other students: "Don't mess with Chrétien!"<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._51_13-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._51-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> When asked in an interview by his biographer Lawrence Martin what subject he was best at in high school, Chrétien replied: "It was street fighting that I was best at".<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> Despite the thuggish image that he cultivated at Séminaire Saint-Joseph, Chrétien's grades were high, with an education that focused mostly on Catholic theology, the classics, philosophy, and French. When Chrétien graduated from Séminaire Saint-Joseph, Duplessis came to address the class and upon meeting Chrétien asked him if his grandfather was François Chrétien, who once served as mayor of St-Étiene-des-Grès, and if his father was Wellie Chrétien. Upon receiving affirmative answers to both questions, the premier said with disgust, "Then you're a damn <i>rouge</i>".<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later at Laval, Chrétien protested the fact that the law faculty gave the <i>Revised Statutes of Quebec</i> free to Union Nationale students while Liberal students had to pay $10 for it, which led him and another student whose family was well connected to meet Duplessis in his office.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Duplessis told Chrétien the Union Nationale only rewarded those who had "faith", and if he wanted the book for free, then he should have had "faith", noting that there were no "rights" in Quebec as he was "Le Chef" ("<i>the boss</i>").<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> At Laval, Chrétien became active in the Young Liberals, becoming president as no one else wanted the job as most students were too frightened to antagonize the Union Nationale.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In 1958 he attended the Liberal convention in Ottawa that chose Lester Pearson as the party's leader, and where Chrétien supported <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin_Sr." title="Paul Martin Sr.">Paul Martin Sr.</a><sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien later drew attention to his humble origins, calling himself "le petit gars de Shawinigan,"<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> or the "little guy from Shawinigan". In his youth he suffered from an attack of <a href="/wiki/Bell%27s_palsy" title="Bell&#39;s palsy">Bell's palsy</a>, permanently leaving one side of his face partially paralyzed.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien used this in his first Liberal leadership campaign, saying that he was "One politician who didn't talk out of both sides of his mouth." He is also deaf in one ear.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 10, 1957, he married <a href="/wiki/Aline_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Aline Chrétien">Aline Chaîné</a>, whom he had met when he was 18 and she was 16. They had three children: France (b. 1958), Hubert (b. 1965) and Michel (b. 1968), who was adopted in 1970. <a href="/wiki/France_Chr%C3%A9tien_Desmarais" title="France Chrétien Desmarais">France Chrétien Desmarais</a>, who is a lawyer, is married to <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Desmarais" title="André Desmarais">André Desmarais</a>, the son of <a href="/wiki/Paul_Desmarais,_Sr." class="mw-redirect" title="Paul Desmarais, Sr.">Paul Desmarais, Sr.</a>, and the president and co-chief executive officer of his father's company, <a href="/wiki/Power_Corporation_of_Canada" title="Power Corporation of Canada">Power Corporation</a>, based in Montreal, Canada. Reflecting Chrétien's poor relations with the Catholic church, the local priest in Shawinigan, Father Auger, refused to marry Chrétien in his church, saying only <i>bleus</i> (blues, i.e. Union Nationale supporters) were welcome in his church and <i>rouges</i> (reds, i.e. Liberals) were not.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._89_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._89-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chrétien practised law at the Shawinigan firm of <a href="/wiki/Alexandre_G%C3%A9linas" title="Alexandre Gélinas">Alexandre Gélinas</a> and Joe Lafond<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> until he was first elected to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons of Canada</a> as a Liberal from the riding of <a href="/wiki/Saint-Maurice%E2%80%93Lafl%C3%A8che" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint-Maurice–Laflèche">Saint-Maurice–Laflèche</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1963_Canadian_federal_election" title="1963 Canadian federal election">1963 election</a>. He represented this Shawinigan-based riding, renamed <a href="/wiki/Saint-Maurice_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Saint-Maurice (federal electoral district)">Saint-Maurice</a> in 1968, for all but eight of the next 41 years. The riding had been won by the <a href="/wiki/Social_Credit_Party_of_Canada" title="Social Credit Party of Canada">Social Credit</a> party in the 1962 election, and Chrétien won the Liberal nomination for the 1963 election as the previous Liberal member of Parliament (MP) decided to retire.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien won the election by portraying the Social Credit MP <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Lamy" title="Gérard Lamy">Gérard Lamy</a> as a "buffoon" who made French-Canadians look stupid.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Early in his career, Chrétien was described by <a href="/wiki/Dalton_Camp" title="Dalton Camp">Dalton Camp</a> as looking like "the driver of the getaway car," a condescending assessment which stuck with him, and which was often cited by journalists and others throughout his career, and usually considering his eventual success. The only committee assignment he requested, and obtained, during his first term was to the Finance Committee.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Shortly before the <a href="/wiki/1965_Canadian_federal_election" title="1965 Canadian federal election">1965 election</a>, Chrétien very briefly served as <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_secretary" title="Parliamentary secretary">parliamentary secretary</a> to Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Lester_B._Pearson" title="Lester B. Pearson">Lester B. Pearson</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._163_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._163-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> When Pearson recruited his "Three Wise Men" consisting of <a href="/wiki/Jean_Marchand" title="Jean Marchand">Jean Marchand</a>, <a href="/wiki/G%C3%A9rard_Pelletier" title="Gérard Pelletier">Gérard Pelletier</a> and <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> into the cabinet, Chrétien was disappointed at being bypassed, telling Pearson he deserved to be promoted to the cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._163_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._163-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Starting in 1966, he served for a more substantial period of time as the parliamentary secretary to <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_(Canada)" title="Minister of Finance (Canada)">Minister of Finance</a> <a href="/wiki/Mitchell_Sharp" title="Mitchell Sharp">Mitchell Sharp</a>. Sharp was to serve as Chrétien's mentor and patron, helping him rise through the ranks.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1967, Chrétien visited western Canada for the first time, which he was curious to see.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Vancouver" title="Vancouver">Vancouver</a> he declared in a speech about the demands for more powers for Quebec being made by Union Nationale Premier <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Johnson_Sr." title="Daniel Johnson Sr.">Daniel Johnson</a> that "those who are in favour of a special status [for Quebec] are often separatists who don't want to admit they are separatists", which caused an uproar in Quebec, with Johnson saying he just wanted more powers for Quebec, not independence.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> When the French President <a href="/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle" title="Charles de Gaulle">Charles de Gaulle</a> in a speech during his visit to Montreal said "<i><a href="/wiki/Vive_le_Qu%C3%A9bec_libre" title="Vive le Québec libre">Vive le Québec libre</a>!</i>" ("Long Live A Free Quebec!") and compared the Quiet Revolution to the Liberation of France from the <a href="/wiki/Nazi" class="mw-redirect" title="Nazi">Nazis</a>, Chrétien in a cabinet meeting demanded that the government order de Gaulle to leave Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Joins_cabinet">Joins cabinet</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Joins cabinet"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chrétien was appointed minister without portfolio in April 1967 and then <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_National_Revenue_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of National Revenue (Canada)">minister of national revenue</a> in January 1968, making him a junior minister in the cabinet. During the <a href="/wiki/1968_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1968 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">1968 Liberal leadership race</a>, Chrétien fought hard on behalf of his mentor Sharp, who aspired to lead the Liberal Party.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._184–185_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._184–185-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> When Sharp withdrew from the race, Chrétien followed Sharp in swinging his support behind the man who eventually won the race, Pierre Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._184–185_31-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._184–185-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Trudeau,_Turner,_Chretien,_and_Pearson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg/220px-Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="145" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg/330px-Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/1/1a/Trudeau%2C_Turner%2C_Chretien%2C_and_Pearson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="389" data-file-height="256" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien, second from right as a minister in <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a>'s Cabinet in 1967. From left to right, <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>, Chrétien, and Pearson. All four men served as Prime Ministers of Canada.</figcaption></figure><p> After the <a href="/wiki/1968_Canadian_federal_election" title="1968 Canadian federal election">June 1968 election</a>, Chrétien was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Indian_Affairs_and_Northern_Development_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Indian Affairs and Northern Development (Canada)">minister of Indian affairs and northern development</a>. Trudeau and Chrétien were never close, as the gulf between the intellectual Trudeau and the decidedly non-intellectual Chrétien was too wide, but Trudeau did value Chrétien as an extremely loyal and competent minister, and as a "tough guy" trouble-shooter who could handle difficult assignments.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau and his intellectual advisors in the <a href="/wiki/Office_of_the_Prime_Minister_(Canada)" title="Office of the Prime Minister (Canada)">Prime Minister's Office</a> (PMO) held Chrétien in contempt as someone who spoke French with a working class accent and whose manners were unpolished, but they appreciated his toughness and ability to get things done.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While at Indian Affairs, Chrétien introduced the <a href="/wiki/1969_White_Paper" title="1969 White Paper">1969 White Paper</a>, a proposal to abolish <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Aboriginal_law" title="Canadian Aboriginal law">treaties between Canada and First Nations</a> and related legislation including the <i><a href="/wiki/Indian_Act" title="Indian Act">Indian Act</a></i>. Critics charged that the goal was to assimilate First Nations people into the general Canadian population.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The paper was widely opposed by First Nations groups, and later abandoned. It was the 1969 White Paper that first brought Chrétien to widespread public attention in <a href="/wiki/English_Canada" title="English Canada">English Canada</a>. At a press conference announcing the White Paper, Chrétien openly clashed with Indian activists with one First Nations woman asking Chrétien, "When did we lose our identity?", to which he replied: "When you signed the treaties", which prompted boos and jeers.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._198_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._198-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Another woman from the Iroquois reserve at Brantford asked Chrétien, "How can you come here and ask us to become citizens, when we were here long before you?", noting the Crown had granted the Grand River valley to <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Brant" title="Joseph Brant">Joseph Brant</a> in 1784, to which Chrétien had no reply.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._198_35-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._198-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Cree" title="Cree">Cree</a> activist <a href="/wiki/Harold_Cardinal" title="Harold Cardinal">Harold Cardinal</a> attacked Chrétien and Trudeau for the White Paper in his bestselling 1969 book <i>The Unjust Society</i>, accusing them of "<a href="/wiki/Cultural_genocide" title="Cultural genocide">cultural genocide</a>" against the First Nations.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> To counteract such criticism, Chrétien adopted an <a href="/wiki/Inuit" title="Inuit">Inuit</a> boy from a local orphanage during a 1970 visit to the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territories" title="Northwest Territories">Northwest Territories</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> As Indian Affairs minister, Chrétien fell in love with the far north of Canada, whose beauty moved him, and he vacationed in the north every summer during his time while holding the Indian Affairs portfolio.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the <a href="/wiki/October_Crisis" title="October Crisis">October Crisis</a> of 1970, Chrétien told Trudeau to "act now, explain later", when Trudeau was hesitant to invoke the <i><a href="/wiki/War_Measures_Act" title="War Measures Act">War Measures Act</a></i>. Eighty-five percent of Canadians agreed with the move. In the 1972 election, Chrétien, who was frightened by a near-defeat in 1968, had a friend Antonio Genest win the Progressive Conservative (PC) nomination, and then run a deliberately inept campaign in order to ensure his re-election.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bourassa" title="Robert Bourassa">Robert Bourassa</a>, the Liberal premier of Quebec, was a nationalist who frequently pressed for more devolution of federal powers to his province, making him Trudeau's <i>bête noire</i>, with the two men openly feuding.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1971, when the Bourassa government began the <a href="/wiki/James_Bay_Project" title="James Bay Project">James Bay Project</a> to develop hydro-electric dams on rivers flowing into <a href="/wiki/James_Bay" title="James Bay">James Bay</a>, which was opposed by the local Cree bands who claimed the land slated for development, Chrétien intervened on the side of the Cree.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In a speech Chrétien said Bourassa "could go to hell", stated he did not have the right to build on or flood the land claimed by the Cree, and hired lawyers to argue for the Cree in the courts.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In November 1973, a judge ruled for the Cree, but a few days later the appeals court ruled for Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224_40-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._224-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1974, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_Treasury_Board" title="President of the Treasury Board">President of the Treasury Board</a>; and beginning in 1976, he served as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Industry_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Industry (Canada)">Minister of Industry, Trade and Commerce</a>. At the Treasury Board, Chrétien become known as "Doctor No," as he refused in a brusque manner requests from other ministers for more money for their departments.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The 1970s were a time of rapid inflation, and Chrétien often clashed with public sector unions who demanded wage increases.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> At a time when deficits were rising and the Trudeau government was widely seen as drifting, Chrétien's "tough guy" image won him widespread attention with many in the media presenting him one of the few people in the Trudeau cabinet willing to make tough decisions.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> As industry minister, Chrétien was in charge of the Trudeau government's efforts to "diversify" the economy by trading more with Asia and Europe and less with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._246_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._246-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien often complained the high Canadian dollar hindered his efforts to "diversify" trade and he became known for his belief in the value of a low dollar.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._246_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._246-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> As industry minister, Chrétien moved to the left, being known for his populist policies, imposing tariffs on clothing made abroad to encourage more production in Canada, and having the government fund the development of the <a href="/wiki/Canadair_Challenger" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadair Challenger">Challenger</a> aircraft.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_finance">Minister of finance</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Minister of finance"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1977, following the resignation of Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Donald_Stovel_Macdonald" title="Donald Stovel Macdonald">Donald MacDonald</a>, Chrétien succeeded him. He was the first <a href="/wiki/Francophone" class="mw-redirect" title="Francophone">francophone</a> minister of finance, and remains one of only three francophones to have held that post. Chrétien's time at Finance highlighted his "enforcer" status, namely as someone who often helped to execute Trudeau's policies, but who rarely helped Trudeau to make policy.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._262-266_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._262-266-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> During his time at Finance, Trudeau completely excluded Chrétien from any role in making financial policy, instead expecting Chrétien to simply carry out the policies that he and his advisors at the PMO had decided beforehand without consulting Chrétien at all.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._262-266_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._262-266-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Trudeau was extremely close to the West German Chancellor <a href="/wiki/Helmut_Schmidt" title="Helmut Schmidt">Helmut Schmidt</a>, and during the <a href="/wiki/4th_G7_summit" title="4th G7 summit">1978 G7</a> summit in Bonn, Trudeau had extensive discussions with his friend Schmidt about how best to win re-election in 1979.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Schmidt suggested to Trudeau that he respond to criticism of the deficits he had been running by bringing in some big cuts to spending, an idea that Trudeau took up.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In 1978 Trudeau announced in a press statement $2&#160;billion in cuts without bothering to inform Chrétien beforehand about what he had decided to do, leaving his finance minister looking clueless in the resulting press interview.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien found this experience so humiliating that he seriously considering resigning in protest.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266_49-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien was especially humiliated by the fact that Chancellor Schmidt was better informed of about what was going to happen than he was, which underlined that he was not a member of Trudeau's inner circle.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266_49-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._265-266-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Chrétien presented the two federal budgets to the House floor in 1978, one in <a href="/wiki/April_1978_Canadian_federal_budget" title="April 1978 Canadian federal budget">April</a> and the other in <a href="/wiki/November_1978_Canadian_federal_budget" title="November 1978 Canadian federal budget">November</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg/220px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="287" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg/330px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg/440px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien1.jpg 2x" data-file-width="639" data-file-height="835" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien in 1980</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Major_role_in_referendum_campaign">Major role in referendum campaign</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Major role in referendum campaign"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Liberals lost the federal election of May 1979 to a minority Conservative government led by <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>. When Pierre Trudeau regained power in February 1980, he appointed Chrétien as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Justice_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Justice (Canada)">minister of justice and attorney general</a>. In this role, he was a major force in the <a href="/wiki/1980_Quebec_referendum" title="1980 Quebec referendum">1980 Quebec referendum</a>, being one of the main federal representatives "on the ground" during the campaign. His fiery and emotional speeches would enthrall federalist crowds with his blunt warnings of the consequences of separation. During the 1980 referendum, Chrétien fiercely fought behind the scenes with the leader of the Quebec Liberals, <a href="/wiki/Claude_Ryan" title="Claude Ryan">Claude Ryan</a>, who served as the chairman of the <i>non</i> committee about the best course to follow, with Ryan favouring a more Quebec nationalist message as opposed to Chrétien's unabashed Canadian nationalist message.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien delivered an average of six or seven speeches a day during the 1980 referendum all across Quebec and always managed to work in a local reference in every speech.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patriation_of_the_Constitution">Patriation of the Constitution</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Patriation of the Constitution"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chrétien also served as minister of state for social development and minister responsible for constitutional negotiations, playing a significant role in the debates leading to the  <a href="/wiki/Patriation" title="Patriation">patriation</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Canada" title="Constitution of Canada">Constitution of Canada</a> in 1982. On September 28, 1981, the Supreme Court ruled that the federal government could patriate the <a href="/wiki/British_North_America_Acts" title="British North America Acts">British North America Acts</a> without the consent of the provinces, but also ruled this would be "odious".<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien informed the premiers opposing patriation that Ottawa would unilaterally patriate the Constitution, but was willing to talk at a final conference.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> During the resulting First Ministers conference in November 1981, two of the premiers, <a href="/wiki/Allan_Blakeney" title="Allan Blakeney">Allan Blakeney</a>, the New Democratic premier of Saskatchewan, and <a href="/wiki/Sterling_Lyon" title="Sterling Lyon">Sterling Lyon</a>, the Progressive Conservative premier of Manitoba, made it clear that their principal objection to the proposed <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms" title="Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms">Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms</a> was that it undermined the ancient British tradition of <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_sovereignty" title="Parliamentary sovereignty">parliamentary supremacy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Siddiquic_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siddiquic-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Ever since the <a href="/wiki/Glorious_Revolution_of_1688" class="mw-redirect" title="Glorious Revolution of 1688">Glorious Revolution of 1688</a>, the principle had always been that Parliament was the supreme lawmaking body in the land, and both Blakeney and Lyon were concerned that the Charter would give too much power to the courts.
</p><p>Chrétien was the chief negotiator of what would be called the "<a href="/wiki/Kitchen_Accord#The_Kitchen_Accord" class="mw-redirect" title="Kitchen Accord">Kitchen Accord</a>," an agreement which led to the agreement of nine provinces to patriation. In the Kitchen Accord, Chrétien, along with Attorneys-General <a href="/wiki/Roy_McMurtry" title="Roy McMurtry">Roy McMurtry</a> of Ontario and <a href="/wiki/Roy_Romanow" title="Roy Romanow">Roy Romanow</a> of Saskatchewan, came up with the compromise of <a href="/wiki/Section_33_of_the_Canadian_Charter_of_Rights_and_Freedoms" title="Section 33 of the Canadian Charter of Rights and Freedoms">Section 33</a>, the so-called "notwithstanding clause," allowing Parliament and provincial legislatures to overrule the courts in Charter cases.<sup id="cite_ref-Siddiquic_54-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siddiquic-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien remembered that Trudeau "hated" the idea of Section 33 and that he had to tell him: "Pierre, if you don't take the notwithstanding clause, you don't have the Charter."<sup id="cite_ref-Siddiquic_54-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Siddiquic-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Trudeau only accepted Section 33 when Ontario Premier <a href="/wiki/Bill_Davis" title="Bill Davis">Bill Davis</a>, who together with New Brunswick Premier <a href="/wiki/Richard_Hatfield" title="Richard Hatfield">Richard Hatfield</a>, were the only premiers supporting the federal government, phoned him to say he would not support Trudeau in London if Trudeau did not accept Section 33, which Chrétien remembered changed Trudeau's attitude completely.<sup id="cite_ref-Faguy_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Faguy-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In a 2012 interview, Chrétien defended the controversial Section 33, saying: "Because some would argue that in a society the elected people have to be supreme — not judges — and I subscribe to that. Look at what happened in the United States where the judges reign according to their so-called philosophy. That is not the tradition here."<sup id="cite_ref-Faguy_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Faguy-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> All of the English-speaking premiers accepted the compromise of Section 33; Quebec Premier <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_L%C3%A9vesque" title="René Lévesque">René Lévesque</a> did not. Chrétien's role in the dealings would not be forgotten in his native province of Quebec (although the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> ruled that Quebec was bound by it). One of Trudeau's aides, <a href="/wiki/Barry_Strayer" title="Barry Strayer">Barry Strayer</a>, later said about Chrétien's role in the constitutional battle: "He was able to contemplate compromises that Trudeau would not have been able to. Everybody saw him as a honest broker. Without him you could argue it would not have happened".<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_of_energy">Minister of energy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Minister of energy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1982, Chrétien was appointed <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Energy,_Mines_and_Resources_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Energy, Mines and Resources (Canada)">Minister of Energy, Mines and Resources</a>. As energy minister, Chrétien was in charge of enforcing the <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Program" title="National Energy Program">National Energy Program</a> (NEP), a role that helped to make him a hated figure in Alberta.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien himself was doubtful about the value of the NEP, saying at the time of his appointment as energy minister that "We've got to back off on the NEP without destroying our credibility" but upon learning that Trudeau and his right-hand man, Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Marc_Lalonde" title="Marc Lalonde">Marc Lalonde</a>, were in favor of continuing the NEP, Chrétien decided to fall in line rather than risk his chances of one day winning the Liberal leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's battles with Alberta Premier <a href="/wiki/Peter_Lougheed" title="Peter Lougheed">Peter Lougheed</a> over the NEP helped to confirm his disdain for provincial politicians whom he saw as petty people only interested in their own provinces at the expense of the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1984:_First_leadership_bid_and_relationship_with_Turner">1984: First leadership bid and relationship with Turner</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: 1984: First leadership bid and relationship with Turner"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pierre_Trudeau_(1975).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg/150px-Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg/225px-Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ae/Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg/300px-Pierre_Trudeau_%281975%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1088" data-file-height="1509" /></a><figcaption>When Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a> announced his retirement in 1984, Chrétien ran for the leadership of the Liberal Party by portraying himself as the candidate who would best continue Trudeau's policies and defend his legacy.</figcaption></figure>
<p>After Trudeau announced his retirement in early 1984 as Liberal Party leader and prime minister, Chrétien was one of the candidates seeking the leadership of the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal Party of Canada</a>. The experience was a hard one for Chrétien, as many of his longtime Cabinet allies supported the bid of <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>, who was viewed as more electable, much to Chrétien's intense disappointment.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> During the leadership race in the spring of 1984, Chrétien ran as the defender of the Trudeau era and promised to continue all of Trudeau's policies, unlike Turner, who promised a break with Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> During the leadership race, Chrétien presented himself as a folksy leftish populist and mocked Turner as a right-wing <a href="/wiki/Bay_Street" title="Bay Street">Bay Street</a> snob who was out of touch with ordinary people.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._332_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._332-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien opposed Turner that the national deficit was not a problem and arguing in a speech, "We have to use the deficit to keep the dignity of our people."<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._332_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._332-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien attracted larger and more enthusiastic crowds than anything that Turner ever managed, but most of the Liberal Party establishment had rallied to Turner when he announced his candidacy in March 1984, which proved to be an insurmountable handicap for Chrétien.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien was thought to be a <a href="/wiki/Dark_horse" title="Dark horse">dark horse</a> until the end but lost on the second ballot to Turner at the <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">leadership convention</a> that June. The Liberal Party president at the time, <a href="/wiki/Iona_Campagnolo" title="Iona Campagnolo">Iona Campagnolo</a>, introduced Chrétien as "Second on the ballot, but first in our hearts." Turner appointed Chrétien <a href="/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Canada" title="Deputy Prime Minister of Canada">deputy prime minister</a> and <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_External_Affairs" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for External Affairs">secretary of state for external affairs</a>.
</p><p>After winning the leadership race, Turner wanted a to forge a reconciliation with Chrétien to lead a united party into the coming general election and so asked Chrétien what terms he would accept.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._339–340_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._339–340-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien, angry about losing the leadership race, asked for terms that he knew that Turner could never give him. Chrétien demanded to be appointed <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>, with control of patronage and organization in Quebec. Turner had already promised the position to <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Ouellet" title="André Ouellet">André Ouellet</a> in exchange for support in the leadership race.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._339–340_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._339–340-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Simpson_pp._15-28_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Simpson_pp._15-28-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> That would have forced Turner to break his promise to Ouellet and so Turner compromised by creating a troika to run Liberal operations in Quebec of Chrétien, Ouellet, and Lalonde.<sup id="cite_ref-Simpson_pp._15-28_65-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Simpson_pp._15-28-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> The troika was a sham, and during the 1984 general election, the three members of the troika spent more time feuding with one another than in combating the Conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-Simpson_pp._15-28_65-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Simpson_pp._15-28-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien's demand for the Quebec lieutenancy was not the only thing that divided him from Turner. Almost immediately, Chrétien and Turner clashed over the issue of an early election. Chrétien advised Turner not to ask the <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">governor general</a> to dissolve Parliament but to keep Parliament in session for the fall of 1984 to give the government a record to run on in a winter election in early 1985. An election had to be called no later than February 1985 since the last election had been in February 1980.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Since Turner believed that a boost in the polls after he became prime minister in late June 1984 justified asking for Parliament to be dissolved for an election in September 1984, Chrétien's advice was disregarded.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="1984.E2.80.931993:_Liberals_in_opposition"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1984–1993:_Liberals_in_opposition">1984–1993: Liberals in opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: 1984–1993: Liberals in opposition"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Relations between Chrétien and Turner were strained, especially after the Liberals had been severely defeated in the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 election</a>. He was one of only 17 Liberal MPs elected from Quebec (the party had won 74 out of 75 seats in 1980). He was also one of only four Liberal MPs from the province elected from a riding outside <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>.
</p><p>Chrétien was a major focal point of dissatisfaction with Turner, with many polls showing his popularity. His 1985 book, <i>Straight from the Heart</i>, was an instant bestseller that recounted his early life in Shawinigan, his years spent in the House of Commons of Canada as both a member of Parliament and Cabinet minister, and his failed 1984 leadership bid.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1986:_Temporarily_leaves_politics">1986: Temporarily leaves politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: 1986: Temporarily leaves politics"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In February 1986, Chrétien, whose relations with Turner were very poor, resigned his seat and left public life for a time. On February 27, 1986, Chrétien, accompanied by his special executive assistant <a href="/wiki/Jean_Carle" title="Jean Carle">Jean Carle</a>, went to Turner's office to hand in his resignation.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> Turner forced Chrétien to wait a considerable period of time during which Carle broke down in tears, and Chrétien was visibly angry when Turner finally received them, making for a tense and barely civil meeting.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's resignation was largely motivated by his desire to organise against Turner better in the <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a>, which was due in the fall of 1986.<sup id="cite_ref-Duffy,_John_p._321_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Duffy,_John_p._321-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> Now working in the private sector again, Chrétien sat on the boards of several corporations, including the Power Corporation of Canada subsidiary Consolidated Bathurst, the <a href="/wiki/Toronto-Dominion_Bank" title="Toronto-Dominion Bank">Toronto-Dominion Bank</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Brick_Furniture" class="mw-redirect" title="Brick Furniture">Brick Warehouse Corporation</a>. Chrétien professed to be retired from politics, but he told reporters within days of his retirement, "I will always be a politician. I love politics."<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Crucially, Chrétien did not disband the campaign organization that he founded in 1984, which suggested that his retirement had always been intended to be temporary.
</p><p>In November 1986, when the Liberals held a leadership review, Chrétien attempted to organize against Turner, which led to a bruising battle between factions loyal to the two men.<sup id="cite_ref-Duffy,_John_p._321_70-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Duffy,_John_p._321-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> To topple Turner, Chrétien used Turner's penchant for heavy drinking to spread rumors that Turner was an alcoholic who was simply too drunk most of the time to lead the Liberals to power effectively.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien formally claimed to be neutral on the question of the leadership review of Turner's management of the Liberal Party, but behind the scenes, he lobbied as many Liberal MPs and senators as possible for their support in bringing down Turner.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> The intense emotions stirred up by the 1986 leadership review were well-illustrated when Chrétien arrived to vote in the review, which led a chaotic scene on the convention floor at the <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Convention_Centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottawa Convention Centre">Ottawa Convention Centre</a>, as pro-Turner and pro-Chrétien Liberals fought one another with their fists, which led to the police being called to end the violence.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> Turner won the leadership review, earning about 75% of the delegate vote. Many Liberals believed that if only Chrétien had won the 1984 convention instead of Turner, they would not have lost the 1984 election or at least have lost it less badly.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988 election</a>, the Liberals only experienced a moderate recovery, doubling the number of seats they won in 1984. <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservative</a> Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Brian_Mulroney" title="Brian Mulroney">Brian Mulroney</a> won a second consecutive <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a>.  Turner losing for a second time led him to announce his resignation as Liberal leader in 1989, triggering the <a href="/wiki/1990_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1990 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">June 1990 Liberal leadership election</a>. Chrétien returned to politics in 1990 for the purpose of becoming leader.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1990:_Returns_to_politics_and_wins_Liberal_leadership">1990: Returns to politics and wins Liberal leadership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: 1990: Returns to politics and wins Liberal leadership"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Paul_martin_2004.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Paul_martin_2004.jpg/150px-Paul_martin_2004.jpg" decoding="async" width="150" height="190" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Paul_martin_2004.jpg/225px-Paul_martin_2004.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1f/Paul_martin_2004.jpg/300px-Paul_martin_2004.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1194" data-file-height="1513" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a> was Chrétien's main opponent for the leadership of the Liberal Party in 1990. The campaign between them started a political rivalry that would endure throughout and beyond Chrétien's premiership.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Chrétien announced that he would run for the party leadership at the June 1990 Liberal leadership convention in <a href="/wiki/Calgary" title="Calgary">Calgary</a>, <a href="/wiki/Alberta" title="Alberta">Alberta</a>. At a press conference in Ottawa on January 23, 1990, he declared that he would run to be the leader and proudly stated that the day would be remembered as the beginning of the "Chrétien era" in Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's principal opponent, <a href="/wiki/Paul_Martin" title="Paul Martin">Paul Martin</a>, was generally seen as the ideological heir to Turner, while Chrétien was seen to be the ideological heir to Trudeau. The fact that most of the Liberals who had supported Turner in the 1980s supported Martin in 1990 confirmed Chrétien's disdain for Martin, whom he saw as a Bay Street "big shot," like Turner.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The most controversial issue facing Canada during the first half of 1990 was the <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a>. It proposed a set of constitutional amendments that would have seen a significant devolution of federal powers to the provinces and included a clause that would have recognized Quebec as a "<a href="/wiki/Distinct_society" title="Distinct society">distinct society</a>" within Canada. Chrétien had announced in a speech in January 1990 that he was an opponent of Meech Lake but stated that he would support the accord with amendments such as scrapping the controversial "distinct society" clause as written; having the preamble to the constitution instead declare that Quebec was a "distinct society"; and adding a new clause saying if any conflict arose between the constitutional recognition of Quebec as a "distinct society" and the Charter of Rights and Freedoms, the latter would always prevail.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._15_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._15-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> The "distinct society" clause theoretically could have been the basis of a wide-ranging devolution of federal power since the clause might have empowered the Quebec government to pass any law short of secession to protect the "distinct society." That made the clause very popular in Quebec and arose much passionate opposition by many quarters in English Canada. In a much-discussed essay, Trudeau had warned that giving Quebec the constitutional right to be a "distinct society" would mean that Quebec could quite legally start to expel its anglophone minority. Chrétien's proposed amendments would have meant that the constitution would have recognized Quebec as a "distinct society" but effectively gutted any attempt to use that to grant any special powers to Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._15_77-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._15-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> In private, Chrétien opposed Meech Lake, but the accord was extremely popular in Quebec and so running as an out-and-out opponent of Meech was judged to be too risky politically. That caused Chrétien's public conditional opposition.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> Meech Lake placed Chrétien in a difficult position, as it was very popular in Quebec and loathed by the Trudeau wing of the Liberals, both of whose support Chrétien needed.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien tried to avoid talking about Meech as much as possible, which was a minefield issue for him; he instead stuck to generalities about national unity.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> Martin, by contrast, had declared himself an unconditional supporter of Meech Lake as it was; he was also quite willing to talk about his support.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien's key campaign man was <a href="/wiki/Jim_Karygiannis" title="Jim Karygiannis">Jim Karygiannis</a>, who specialized in signing up immigrants to serve as delegates for Chrétien. He signed 9,500 immigrants as Chrétien delegates between January and June 1990. In large part because of Karygiannis and his team, Chrétien had by late April 1990 signed up 1,500 delegates, which made him the clear front-runner compared to Martin's 500 delegates.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p19_82-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p19-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A key moment in the race took place at an all-candidates debate in Montreal on June 2, 1990. The discussion quickly turned to the Meech Lake Accord, which had emerged as the major policy issue that divided Martin and Chrétien. At the debate on June 2, 1990, Martin attempted to force Chrétien to abandon the latter's nuanced position on Meech Lake and speak out for or against it by arguing that Chrétien's position of opposing Meech Lake as it is but being willing to support it with amendments was trying to have it both ways.<sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> When Chrétien refused to endorse Meech as it was, young Liberal delegates crowding the hall began to chant <i>vendu</i> ("sellout" in French), "you're selling out to the Anglos" and "<a href="/wiki/Judas_Iscariot" title="Judas Iscariot">Judas</a>" at Chrétien.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup> One of Chrétien's aides frenetically asked that Martin "get the fuck out there and do something," as the Young Liberals continued shouting abuse at Chrétien, to his clear discomfort, only to be told that Quebec youth were "hotheads," whom nobody could control.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p24_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p24-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> In private, Chrétien was deeply enraged by the incident, claimed that the delegates shouting <i>vendu</i> at him were actually Martin supporters from Toronto, and charged that their poor French had betrayed that they had not been from Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p24_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p24-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Martin denied involvement in "coordinating" any response from the floor or a similar outburst by his supporters at the convention.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p24_85-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p24-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> Ultimately, Chrétien defeated Martin on the first and only ballot, but Chrétien's position on Meech Lake had irreversibly damaged his reputation in his home province.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leader_of_the_Official_Opposition">Leader of the Official Opposition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Leader of the Official Opposition"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As his victory at the convention on June 23, 1990, occurred on the same day that the Meech Lake accord died, Chrétien was heavily criticized in the Quebec media for his opposition to Meech Lake. Photographs of Chrétien embracing Newfoundland Premier <a href="/wiki/Clyde_Kirby_Wells" class="mw-redirect" title="Clyde Kirby Wells">Clyde Wells</a>, a prominent opponent of Meech at the convention attracted much negative comment in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> His leadership was also shaken by the defection from the caucus of francophone MPs (and Martin loyalists) <a href="/wiki/Jean_Lapierre" title="Jean Lapierre">Jean Lapierre</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gilles_Rocheleau" title="Gilles Rocheleau">Gilles Rocheleau</a>, who claimed that they could not serve under the anti-Meech Chrétien and so left to join the newly founded Bloc Québécois. In a <a href="/wiki/1990_Laurier%E2%80%94Sainte-Marie_federal_by-election" title="1990 Laurier—Sainte-Marie federal by-election">by-election</a> for <a href="/wiki/Laurier%E2%80%94Sainte-Marie" title="Laurier—Sainte-Marie">Laurier—Sainte-Marie</a> on August 13, 1990, Chrétien's hand-picked candidate, <a href="/wiki/Denis_Coderre" title="Denis Coderre">Denis Coderre</a> was badly defeated by the Bloc Québécois's <a href="/wiki/Gilles_Duceppe" title="Gilles Duceppe">Gilles Duceppe</a>, costing the Liberals a riding that they had held since the 1917 election; many attributed this to Chrétien's opposition to the Meech Lake Accord.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p30_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p30-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> Upon becoming Liberal leader, Chrétien appointed his friend <a href="/wiki/Eddie_Goldenberg" title="Eddie Goldenberg">Eddie Goldenberg</a> as his chief of staff, and formed a leadership team comprising John Rae and David Zussman as his policy advisors, his "surrogate son" Jean Carle as his special executive assistant, <a href="/wiki/Warren_Kinsella" title="Warren Kinsella">Warren Kinsella</a> as his media adviser, and <a href="/wiki/George_Radwanski" title="George Radwanski">George Radwanski</a> as his speech-writer.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._198_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._198-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> All of the Chrétien leadership team that was created in 1990 were later to play prominent roles in the omnipotent Prime Minister's Office during Chrétien's time as prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._198_88-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._198-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 1990, Chrétien seeing a chance to make a strong impression on public opinion after a shaky start as a leader reaped a major windfall after Mulroney introduced an unpopular <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Canada)">Goods and Services Tax</a> (GST), which Chrétien decided to vigorously oppose.<sup id="cite_ref-Tanguay-p217-239_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tanguay-p217-239-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> Traditionally in Canada, the government had levied a 13.5 percent Manufacturer's Sales Tax (MST), which was paid by manufacturers, who passed on the cost of the tax to consumers in the form of higher prices. Since foreign manufacturers did not pay the MST, this placed Canadian companies at a competitive disadvantage in their home market, and to compensate the government had levied tariffs on manufactured imports to maintain a level playing field. When the free trade agreement with the United States came into effect in 1989, the government could no longer levy tariffs on American imports, which led to furious complaints from Canadian industry about having to compete with American companies who did not pay the MST. To save Canadian industry and the jobs of those Canadians employed in manufacturing from being destroyed by American competition, the Mulroney government decided in late 1989 to abolish the MST and replace it with the 7 percent GST, whose costs would be borne by consumers. On the proposed GST, Chrétien was torn between his belief that the GST was economically necessary vs. his desire to score political points by opposing a proposed tax that most Canadians hated, and as such, he was initially vague about where he stood about the GST.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> It was only in September 1990 after months of vacillation did Chrétien finally make up his mind to oppose the GST.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien's decision to oppose the GST in 1990 was taken for reasons of political expediency rather than for principle, namely that Chrétien needed an issue to oppose the government on that would allow him to connect with the public; sources close to Chrétien were later to claim that he had wanted to support the GST bill, but had been forced by his caucus against his will to oppose it.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_91-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> At a Liberal event in the fall of 1990, Chrétien stated that if he became prime minister "the Mulroney GST will disappear", going on to say: "I am opposed to the GST. I have always been opposed to it. And I will be opposed to it, always".<sup id="cite_ref-Tanguay-p217-239_89-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tanguay-p217-239-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> To capitalize on widespread public dislike on the proposed GST, Chrétien ordered the Liberal-dominated <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> to defeat the GST bill in late September 1990, leading Mulroney on September 27, 1990, to appoint 8 Conservative senators to give the Tories a majority using a never-before-used section of the <i>Constitution Act</i>, the so-called "Deadlock Clause".<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> At that point, Chrétien ordered the Liberal senators to <a href="/wiki/Filibuster" title="Filibuster">filibuster</a> the GST bill, reducing the Senate to scenes of chaos for the entire fall of 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> On October 24, 1990, a poll revealed that the Liberals had fallen behind the New Democrats, which Chrétien admitted in an interview might have something to do with the scenes of obstructionist, often childish behaviour by the Liberal senators.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 1990, Chrétien returned to the House of Commons after winning a <a href="/wiki/1990_Beaus%C3%A9jour_federal_by-election" title="1990 Beauséjour federal by-election">by-election</a> in the safe Liberal riding of <a href="/wiki/Beaus%C3%A9jour_(electoral_district)" title="Beauséjour (electoral district)">Beauséjour, New Brunswick</a>. The incumbent, <a href="/wiki/Fernand_Robichaud" title="Fernand Robichaud">Fernand Robichaud</a>, stood down in Chrétien's favour, which is traditional practice when a newly elected party leader does not have a seat in House of Commons. Initially, Chrétien had planned to wait until the next general election before running, but was advised by <a href="/wiki/Herb_Gray" title="Herb Gray">Herb Gray</a> that: "To have credibility, you're got to be in the House. You can't afford to wait two more years until a general election".<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p31_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p31-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> Gray's appeal changed Chrétien's mind about when to seek a seat in the House of Commons. For much of 1991–92, Chrétien found himself emotionally exhausted after his adopted son Michel was charged with kidnapping, rape, and sodomy against a Montreal woman and was convicted.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._53_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._53-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Michel Chrétien suffered from <a href="/wiki/Fetal_alcohol_spectrum_disorder" title="Fetal alcohol spectrum disorder">fetal alcohol spectrum disorder</a> and had a long history of legal trouble.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._53_97-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._53-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 1991, Chrétien first gave his views about how best to end the recession which had begun in 1990. Chrétien argued that the answer was a policy of slow <a href="/wiki/Devaluation" title="Devaluation">devaluation</a> where the dollar would be allowed to decline against other major world currencies, which would have the effect of both pricing out foreign imports and by giving Canadian firms a competitive advantage in world markets, boost exports.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> However, Chrétien concluded that his planned export offensive powered by a low dollar would come to nothing if other nations maintained tariffs to keep Canadian goods out of their markets.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> In order to make his plans to export Canada back into prosperity work, Chrétien decided that the solution was <a href="/wiki/Globalization" title="Globalization">globalization</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46_98-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Besides for globalization, Chrétien also argued to combat the recession, the federal government needed to make the system of unemployment insurance less generous, and to end the policy of high interest rates maintained by the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Canada" title="Bank of Canada">Bank of Canada</a> Governor <a href="/wiki/John_Crow" title="John Crow">John Crow</a> to achieve his target of zero percent inflation, which Chrétien argued was needlessly crippling the economy.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46_98-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._46-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup>
