</p><p>In November 1991, Chrétien organised a party conference in <a href="/wiki/Aylmer,_Quebec" title="Aylmer, Quebec">Aylmer</a>, Quebec where the Liberals formally disallowed most of the economic nationalism and protectionism of the Pearson-Trudeau years, and instead embraced globalization as the cure for the recession of the early 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._47-48_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_pp._47-48-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> Reflecting the changed emphasis, at the Aylmer conference, the Liberals declared their support for the 1987 free trade agreement with the United States, which the party had famously promised to tear up if they won the 1988 election, and instead Mulroney was now denounced for not going far enough in opening up the economy by signing more free trade agreements with other nations.
</p><p>Chrétien revealed himself to be a staunch "hard federalist" favouring a strong federal government at the expense of the provinces, much along the same lines as his predecessor Trudeau. However unlike Trudeau, Chrétien supported the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a> of August 1992, which proposed devolving federal powers to the provinces and once again recognized Quebec as a "distinct society".<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p55_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p55-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien endorsed the Charlottetown accord on the rather negative grounds that the constitutional debate of the late 1980s-early 1990s were destroying Canada, saying it "was bleeding the nation to death" and that Charlottetown was the best way of ending that debate in order to move political debate back to the economic recession, which had begun in 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p55_100-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p55-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> At a Liberal caucus meeting on September 8, 1992, Chrétien declared that "if we had been the government we would not have made this deal", and that only reason to support Charlottetown was that to reject it would increase support for Quebec separatism.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Progressive Conservative Prime Minister Brian Mulroney began to lose ground in the polls, Chrétien was the major beneficiary. In preparation for the 1993 election, Chrétien won the right to have the final say over riding nominations and to veto any candidate that displeased him.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._56_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._56-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's use of this power caused some protests within the Liberal Party with <a href="/wiki/John_Nunziata" title="John Nunziata">John Nunziata</a> publicly complaining that "The backroom guys have taken control of the party. I guess they think they can muzzle us all".<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._56_102-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._56-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien was so confident that he was going to win the 1993 election that he formed his transition team in October 1992 to prepare for the hand-over of power 13 months before it actually happened.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._58_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._58-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="The_1993_election">The 1993 election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: The 1993 election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Mulroney announced his retirement in February, and was succeeded by Minister of National Defence <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a> in June. Campbell pulled the PCs to within a few percentage points of the Liberals by the time <a href="/wiki/Dropping_the_writ" class="mw-redirect" title="Dropping the writ">the writs were dropped</a> in September.
</p><p>On September 19, Chrétien released the Liberal platform. The 112-p. document, <i>Creating Opportunity</i>, quickly became known as the <a href="/wiki/Red_Book_(Liberal_Party_of_Canada)" title="Red Book (Liberal Party of Canada)">Red Book</a> because of its bright red cover. Paul Martin, the man who led the team that produced the Red Book was less complimentary about the Red Book in private as during his time in office as Finance Minister, he was often reported to have said: "Don't tell me about the Red Book, I wrote the damn thing, and I know that it is a lot of crap!".<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey-p265_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey-p265-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Liberals promised to remove the GST, which had previously been imposed by the Conservatives.<sup id="cite_ref-Tanguay-p217-239_89-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tanguay-p217-239-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Sheila_Copps" title="Sheila Copps">Sheila Copps</a> famously promising to resign within a year of taking office if the GST was not repealed.<sup id="cite_ref-Tanguay-p217-239_89-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tanguay-p217-239-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien promised to renegotiate the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a> (NAFTA), and reform to the <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_insurance" class="mw-redirect" title="Unemployment insurance">unemployment insurance</a> system. The Liberals were in favour of a free trade agreement for North America in principle, but went on to accuse Mulroney of having given away too much to the Americans and Mexicans when he signed NAFTA in 1992, and stated that the Liberal government would renegotiate NAFTA on more favourable terms to Canada within six months of taking office. Failing that, Chrétien promised that Canada would renounce NAFTA. The main emphasis was on a promise to spend $6&#160;billion on improving infrastructure in a Keynesian move to fight the recession of the early 1990s.<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> As regarding the debt situation, Chrétien promised to reduce Canada's deficit to 3 per cent of GDP (the same deficit to GDP ratio required to enter the European Union) within three years of taking office.<sup id="cite_ref-Grady_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grady-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien made it clear that the 3 percent deficit to GDP ratio would apply only to the federal government, whereas the <a href="/wiki/Maastricht_Treaty" title="Maastricht Treaty">Maastricht Treaty</a> of 1991 which set out the 3 percent deficit to GDP ratio in order to enter the European Union stated that this applied to all levels of government.<sup id="cite_ref-Grady_106-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grady-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> The Liberal government promised to achieve its goal of reducing the deficit to 3 percent of the GDP by cancelling the contract to replace the Sea King helicopters, privatizing <a href="/wiki/Toronto_Pearson_International_Airport" title="Toronto Pearson International Airport">Toronto Pearson Airport</a>, and by eliminating unspecified "waste" in the government. After the 3 percent target had been achieved within the first three years of taking office, Chrétien promised the deficit would be eliminated at some unspecified time in the future. Martin wanted to promise to eliminate the deficit altogether, but had been overruled by Chrétien, who had wanted to present the Liberals as the "caring" party that would defend social programs, unlike the "heartless" Conservatives and the Reform Party who Chrétien claimed wanted to eliminate the deficit within two or three years by gutting social programs with no thought for any suffering that this might cause.<sup id="cite_ref-107" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien claimed in his campaign speeches that Reform's plans for eliminating the deficit within two or three years of taking office would cause at least a 25 percent unemployment rate, if not higher, which Chrétien claimed starkly in a series of speeches would cause a bloody "revolution".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien had personally chosen the target of reducing the deficit to 3 percent of GDP as it made the Liberals seemed fiscally responsible while at the same time promised that the Liberals would not inflict too much economic pain to achieve that fiscal responsibility.<sup id="cite_ref-Grady_106-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Grady-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> One Liberal candidate <a href="/wiki/Herb_Dhaliwal" title="Herb Dhaliwal">Herb Dhaliwal</a> recalled that for Chrétien at time of the 1993 election that the national deficit was not a major issue and that: "His attitude was that the deficit is ok as long as you can manage it".<sup id="cite_ref-:2_108-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:2-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1210818076">.mw-parser-output .quotebox{background-color:#F9F9F9;border:1px solid #aaa;box-sizing:border-box;padding:10px;font-size:88%;max-width:100%}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft{margin:.5em 1.4em .8em 0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright{margin:.5em 0 .8em 1.4em}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.centered{overflow:hidden;position:relative;margin:.5em auto .8em auto}.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatleft span,.mw-parser-output .quotebox.floatright span{font-style:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox>blockquote{margin:0;padding:0;border-left:0;font-family:inherit;font-size:inherit}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-title{background-color:#F9F9F9;text-align:center;font-size:110%;font-weight:bold}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote>:first-child{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote:last-child>:last-child{margin-bottom:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:before{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" “ ";vertical-align:-45%;line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox-quote.quoted:after{font-family:"Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:bold;font-size:large;color:gray;content:" ” ";line-height:0}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .left-aligned{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .right-aligned{text-align:right}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .center-aligned{text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quote-title,.mw-parser-output .quotebox .quotebox-quote{display:block}.mw-parser-output .quotebox cite{display:block;font-style:normal}@media screen and (max-width:640px){.mw-parser-output .quotebox{width:100%!important;margin:0 0 .8em!important;float:none!important}}</style><div class="quotebox pullquote floatright" style="width:216px; ;">
<blockquote class="quotebox-quote left-aligned" style="">
<p>But last night, the Conservative Party reached a new low; they tried to make fun of the way I look. God gave me a physical defect, and I accepted that since I'm a kid. &#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; It's true, that I speak on one side of my mouth. I'm not a Tory, I don't speak on both sides of my mouth.
</p>
</blockquote>
<p style="padding-bottom: 0em;"><cite class="left-aligned" style="">—Jean Chretien responding to the "<a href="/wiki/1993_Chr%C3%A9tien_attack_ad" title="1993 Chrétien attack ad">face ad</a>", 1993. <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PikszBkfTHM">Source video</a></cite></p>
</div>
<p>By late September 1993, the Liberals had a double-digit lead in most opinion polls. By October, the Liberals were the favourites to win at least a <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority government</a>. Even at this stage, however, Chrétien's personal approval ratings were far behind those of Campbell. Realizing this, the PC campaign team released a series of ads attacking Chrétien. The ads were viewed as a last-ditch effort to keep the Liberals from winning a majority. The second ad, released on October 14, appeared to mock Chrétien's facial paralysis, and generated a severe backlash from all sides.<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> Even some Tory candidates called for the ad to be removed. Campbell was not directly responsible for the ad, and ordered it off the air over her staff's objections.
</p><p>On October 25, the Liberals were elected to a majority government, winning 177 seats&#160;&#8211;&#32;the third-best performance in the Liberals' history, and their most impressive win since their record of 190 seats in <a href="/wiki/1949_Canadian_federal_election" title="1949 Canadian federal election">1949</a>. The PCs were nearly wiped out, winning only two seats in the worst defeat ever suffered by a governing party at the federal level. Chrétien himself yielded Beauséjour back to Robichaud in order to run in his old riding, Saint-Maurice. However, he was unable to lead the Liberals back to their traditional dominance in Quebec. He was one of only four Liberal MPs elected from that province outside the Montreal area. With few exceptions, most of the support that had switched from the Liberals to the PCs nine years earlier flowed to the Bloc Quebecois, which became the Official Opposition.
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281993.E2.80.932003.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1993–2003)">Prime Minister (1993–2003)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1993–2003)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Premiership_of_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Premiership of Jean Chrétien">Premiership of Jean Chrétien</a></div>
<p>On November 4, 1993, Chrétien became prime minister. While Trudeau, Joe Clark, and Mulroney had been relative political outsiders prior to becoming prime minister, Chrétien had served in every Liberal cabinet since 1965. This experience gave him knowledge of the Canadian parliamentary system, and allowed Chrétien to establish a very centralized government that, although highly efficient, was also lambasted by critics such as <a href="/wiki/Jeffrey_Simpson" title="Jeffrey Simpson">Jeffrey Simpson</a> and the media as being a "friendly dictatorship" and intolerant of internal dissent.<sup id="cite_ref-110" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien liked to present himself as the heir to Trudeau, but his governing style had little in common with the intense bouts of governmental activism that had characterised the Trudeau era. The Chrétien government had a cautious, managerial approach to governing, reacting to issues as they arose, and was otherwise inclined to inactivity.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p97_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p97-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Quebec">Quebec</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Quebec"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1995_Quebec_referendum">1995 Quebec referendum</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: 1995 Quebec referendum"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum" title="1995 Quebec referendum">1995 Quebec referendum</a></div>
<p>One of Chrétien's main concerns in office was separation of the province of Quebec, which was governed by the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_sovereignty_movement" title="Quebec sovereignty movement">sovereigntist</a> <a href="/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Parti Québécois">Parti Québécois</a> for nearly the entirety of his term. When the <a href="/wiki/1995_Quebec_referendum" title="1995 Quebec referendum">1995 Quebec independence referendum</a> began in September, Chrétien was relaxed and confident of victory as polls showed federalist forces were leading by a wide margin.<sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> On October 8, 1995, <a href="/wiki/Lucien_Bouchard" title="Lucien Bouchard">Lucien Bouchard</a> replaced the separatist <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Quebec" title="Premier of Quebec">premier of Quebec</a>, <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Parizeau" title="Jacques Parizeau">Jacques Parizeau</a>, as the <i>de facto</i> chair of the <i>oui</i> committee and, at that point, the support for the <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">oui</i></span> side started to dramatically increase, aided by the <span title="French-language text"><i lang="fr">non</i></span> committee's complacency.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_pp._278-279_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_pp._278-279-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In the weeks leading to the referendum on October 30, 1995, the federal government was seized with fear and panic as polls showing that, under the leadership of Bouchard, the <i>oui</i> side was going to win.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> On October 30, 1995, the federalist <i>non</i> side won by the narrowest of margins, with 50.58%.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath_of_referendum">Aftermath of referendum</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Aftermath of referendum"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On November 5, 1995, six days after the referendum, Chrétien and his wife escaped injury when <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Dallaire" title="André Dallaire">André Dallaire</a>, armed with a knife, broke in the prime minister's official residence at <a href="/wiki/24_Sussex_Drive" title="24 Sussex Drive">24 Sussex Drive</a>. Aline Chrétien shut and locked the bedroom door until security came, while Chrétien held a stone Inuit carving in readiness.<sup id="cite_ref-CE_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CE-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Dallaire was 
a separatist who was angered by the result of the referendum.
</p><p>In the aftermath of the narrow victory in the referendum, Chrétien started in late 1995 a new policy of "tough love", also known as "Plan B", where the federal government sought to discredit Quebec separatism by making it clear to the people of Quebec how difficult it would be to leave Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> Though Chrétien had promised to enshrine recognition of Quebec as a "distinct society" in the constitution in order to win the referendum, this promise was quickly forgotten in the aftermath of victory with Chrétien arguing that the very vocal opposition of Ontario Premier <a href="/wiki/Mike_Harris" title="Mike Harris">Mike Harris</a> to amending the constitution to recognize Quebec as a "distinct society" made that impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Instead, Chrétien had Parliament pass a resolution recognizing Quebec as a "distinct society", which had no constitutional force and was only a symbolic step.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284_117-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> Though Harris's promise to veto any sort of "distinct society" clause in the constitution made fulfilling Chrétien's commitment to put such a clause into the constitution impossible, Chrétien did not seem to champion the idea of a "distinct society" clause with any great conviction.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284_117-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._284-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early 1996, the federal government launched an advertising program to increase the presence of Canada in Quebec, a policy that Chrétien believed would avoid a repeat of the near-defeat of 1995, and was to lead eventually to the <a href="/wiki/Sponsorship_scandal" title="Sponsorship scandal">Sponsorship scandal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._287_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_2010,_p._287-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> As part of his "Plan B" for combatting Quebec separatism, in a speech in January 1996, Chrétien endorsed the idea of partitioning Quebec in the event of a <i>oui</i> vote in another referendum, stating all of the regions of Quebec that voted <i>non</i> would remain part of Canada, regardless of what the Quebec separatists thought.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> On February 15, 1996, Chrétien was confronted by a protester, Bill Clennett, during a walkabout in Hull, Quebec. Chrétien responded with a choke-hold. The press referred to it as the "<a href="/wiki/Shawinigan_Handshake" title="Shawinigan Handshake">Shawinigan handshake</a>" (from the name of his home town).<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Clarity_Act"><i>Clarity Act</i></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Clarity Act"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>After the 1995 referendum very narrowly defeated a proposal on Quebec sovereignty, Chrétien started to champion what eventually become the <i><a href="/wiki/Clarity_Act" title="Clarity Act">Clarity Act</a></i> as part of his "Plan B". In August 1996, the lawyer <a href="/wiki/Guy_Bertrand_(lawyer)" title="Guy Bertrand (lawyer)">Guy Bertrand</a> won a ruling in a Quebec court declaring that the sovereignty question was not just a political matter between the federal and Quebec governments, but also a legal matter subject to court rulings.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._165_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._165-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Following that ruling, Chrétien decided that here was a means of defeating the Quebec sovereignty movement and, in September 1996, ordered the Justice Minister <a href="/wiki/Allan_Rock" title="Allan Rock">Allan Rock</a> to take the question of the legality of Quebec separating to the Supreme Court.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._165_121-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._165-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Dion" title="Stéphane Dion">Stéphane Dion</a> advised Chrétien that, if the federal government won the reference to the Supreme Court as expected, the government should draft a bill stating the precise rules for Quebec to leave—telling Chrétien if the people of Quebec could be shown how difficult it would be to leave, then support for separatism would fall.<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> Along the same lines, Dion started to send much-publicised open letters to Quebec ministers questioning the assumptions behind the separatist case.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In December 1999 the Chrétien government tabled the <i>Clarity Act</i>, which passed Parliament in June 2000. The <i>Clarity Act</i>, which was Chrétien's response to his narrow victory in the 1995 referendum requires that no Canadian government may acknowledge any province's declaration of independence unless a "clear majority" supports a "clear question" about sovereignty in a referendum, as defined by the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament of Canada</a>, and a constitutional amendment is passed. The size of a "clear majority" is not specified in the Act. After the <i>Clarity Act</i> had passed by the House of Commons in February 2000, a poll showed that the federalist forces enjoyed a 15 percent lead in the polls on the question if Quebec should become independent, which Chrétien argued meant that the sovereignty option was now effectively off the table as Bouchard had always said he would only call another referendum if he could obtain "winning conditions", which he plainly did not possess at the moment.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_affairs">Domestic affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Domestic affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In November 1997, the <a href="/wiki/APEC" class="mw-redirect" title="APEC">Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation</a> (APEC) <a href="/wiki/APEC_Canada_1997" title="APEC Canada 1997">summit</a> was held on the <a href="/wiki/University_of_British_Columbia" title="University of British Columbia">University of British Columbia</a> (UBC) campus in Vancouver. Students on UBC's campus protested the meeting of some of these leaders because of their poor human rights practices. One of the leaders most criticized was Indonesian President <a href="/wiki/Suharto" title="Suharto">Suharto</a>. Demonstrators tore down a barrier and were pepper-sprayed by the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Canadian_Mounted_Police" title="Royal Canadian Mounted Police">Royal Canadian Mounted Police</a> (RCMP). Other peaceful demonstrators were subsequently pepper-sprayed as well. There was debate over whether the action was necessary.  In response to Suharto's concerns about his "dignity" being called into question by protests, he had been promised by the Canadian government that no protesters would be allowed to get close and in early August 1997, the RCMP was informed by the PMO that the prime minister did not wish for any "distractions" at the up-coming conference.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p198_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p198-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> On August 7, 2001, the APEC report was issued by Judge <a href="/wiki/Ted_Hughes_(judge)" title="Ted Hughes (judge)">Ted Hughes</a>, which cleared Chrétien of wrongdoing, but stated that Jean Carle of the PMO had improperly pressured the RCMP to attack the protesters.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p328_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p328-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1999, the Anglo-Canadian media magnate <a href="/wiki/Conrad_Black" title="Conrad Black">Conrad Black</a> was due to receive a <a href="/wiki/Peerages_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Peerages in the United Kingdom">British peerage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Two days before Black was to receive his title, Chrétien advised the Queen not to accord Black a title of nobility, citing the 1917 <a href="/wiki/Nickle_Resolution" class="mw-redirect" title="Nickle Resolution">Nickle Resolution</a>, where the Canadian House of Commons asked King George V not to grant any titles of nobility or knighthoods to Canadians, and thereby ensured that Black was not raised to the peerage as he was expecting to be.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> Black, who felt humiliated by this episode, sued Chrétien for what he alleged to be an abuse of power, leading to the legal case of <a href="/wiki/Black_v._Chr%C3%A9tien" class="mw-redirect" title="Black v. Chrétien">Black v. Chrétien</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._231_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._231-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> In 2001, the court ruled in favor of Chrétien, stating it was the prime minister's prerogative to advise the Queen not to raise Canadians to the British peerage if he felt so inclined, and thereforth this was not an abuse of power as Black had claimed.<sup id="cite_ref-130" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> Black gave up his Canadian citizenship to accept the title.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Electoral_affairs">Electoral affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Electoral affairs"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Federal_political_financing_in_Canada" title="Federal political financing in Canada">Federal political financing in Canada</a></div>
<p>In July 2003 Chretien passed a bill to reform the way elections are financed.≥<sup id="cite_ref-young04_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-young04-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> In the previous century, the political parties were largely left to their own devices. After these changes to the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Elections_Act" title="Canada Elections Act">Canada Elections Act</a> (SC 2000), each vote obtained by a party was subsidized. The subsidy entered into effect on January 1, 2004, at $1.75 per vote (indexed to the <a href="/wiki/Consumer_Price_Index" class="mw-redirect" title="Consumer Price Index">Consumer Price Index</a>) as part of a set of amendments made by the <a href="/wiki/37th_Canadian_Parliament" title="37th Canadian Parliament">37th Canadian Parliament</a> to the Canada Elections Act which for the first time set limits on political contributions by individuals and organizations (corporations, unions, non-profit groups). The per-vote subsidy was introduced to replace the reliance of political parties and candidates on corporate, union, and wealthy donors in order to reduce the political influence of such donors.<sup id="cite_ref-Party_subsidy_is_democracy_in_action_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Party_subsidy_is_democracy_in_action-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-pp_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-pp-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup> The law provides a refund for 50% of the expenditure on the most recent election campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-gm1_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gm1-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_issues">Social issues</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Social issues"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1995, the Chrétien government introduced and passed the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Firearms_Registry" title="Canadian Firearms Registry">Canadian Firearms Registry</a>, also called the long-gun registry. This would require the registration of all non-restricted firearms in Canada. This <a href="/wiki/Gun_registry" title="Gun registry">gun registry</a> would document and record information of the firearms, their owners, and their owners' licenses.
</p><p>The government under Chrétien's premiership introduced a new and far-reaching <i><a href="/wiki/Youth_Criminal_Justice_Act" title="Youth Criminal Justice Act">Youth Criminal Justice Act</a></i> in April 2003, which replaced the <i><a href="/wiki/Young_Offenders_Act" title="Young Offenders Act">Young Offenders Act</a></i> and changed the way youths were prosecuted for crimes in Canada. A flurry of major environmental legislation, including the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Protection_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Environmental Protection Act">Canadian Environmental Protection Act</a></i>, <a href="/wiki/National_Marine_Conservation_Area" title="National Marine Conservation Area"><i>National Marine Conservation Areas Act</i></a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Pest_Control_Products_Act" title="Pest Control Products Act">Pest Control Products Act</a></i>, and the <i><a href="/wiki/Species_at_Risk_Act" title="Species at Risk Act">Species at Risk Act</a></i> were enacted. The cooperation of federal, provincial, and municipal governments also enabled Vancouver to win the bid to host the <a href="/wiki/2010_Winter_Olympics" title="2010 Winter Olympics">2010 Winter Olympics</a>.
</p><p>In July 2003, Chrétien reversed his position on gay marriage, which he had previously been opposed to (in 1999 Chrétien had voted for a resolution sponsored by the Reform saying marriage was a union of a man and a woman only).<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> After a Toronto court ruled that laws forbidding homosexual marriage violated the <i>Charter of Rights and Freedoms</i>, legalizing same-sex marriage throughout Ontario, Chrétien embraced the idea of gay marriage and introduced a bill in the House of Commons that would have legalized gay marriage despite the very vocal opposition of the Roman Catholic Church with the bishop of Calgary warning in a sermon that Chrétien's "eternal salvation" was at risk.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Economic policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Chrétien cancelled the privatization of Toronto's Pearson airport.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> The consortium that was due to take ownership of Pearson sued for breach of contract, which led the government to settle out of court in April 1997 for $60&#160;million in damages.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/1994_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1994 Canadian federal budget">The first budget</a> introduced by Martin, in February 1994, was described as a "mild and tame" budget focused only on the target of reducing the deficit to 3 percent of <a href="/wiki/Gross_national_product" class="mw-redirect" title="Gross national product">Gross National Product</a> (GNP) within three years, and brought in modest cuts, mostly to defence spending.<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> Until the terrorist attacks of September 11, 2001, the Chrétien government tended to be hostile towards defence spending with the government's white paper "Defence 94" declaring that in a post-Cold War world there would be less and less need for armed forces, which accordingly meant reduced budgets for the military.<sup id="cite_ref-Bland_pp._964–967_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bland_pp._964–967-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup> Outside of defence spending, there were few cuts in the 1994 budget. In a radio interview with Ron Collister in March 1994, Chrétien stated: "To go to our goal of 3 per cent of GNP, all the cuts have been announced in the budget. There will not be a new round."<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey-p265_104-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey-p265-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> According to the diplomat <a href="/wiki/James_Bartleman" title="James Bartleman">James Bartleman</a>, Chrétien told him in early 1994 that major cuts to government spending outside of defence were out of the question, and instead he hoped that the economy would grow enough on its own that the deficit would disappear without any cuts.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._246_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._246-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's plans in early 1994 for economic growth were to increase exports by embracing globalization and free trade with as many nations as possible, arguing that the export offensive would stimulate the economy out of the early 1990s recession.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._246_143-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey,_Brooke_p._246-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> The 1994 budget was widely criticized by journalists such as <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Coyne" title="Andrew Coyne">Andrew Coyne</a> as useless in even achieving its target of reducing the deficit to 3 percent of GNP within three years, let alone eliminating the deficit, and led to a celebrated clash between Coyne and Martin in the boardroom of <i>The Globe and Mail</i> newspaper.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> In April 1994, interest rates in Canada started a steady rise that would continue until early 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey-p265_104-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey-p265-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien,_1996.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg/250px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="180" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg/375px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg/500px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien%2C_1996.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1062" data-file-height="766" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien in 1996</figcaption></figure>
<p>Chrétien was not keen on making deep cuts to government spending, but given the crisis caused by the skyrocketing interest rates had decided "reluctantly" there was no alternative.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey-p266_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey-p266-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> Once he had decided upon making deeper cuts than he promised, Chrétien proved to be firm supporter of the new course, and supported Martin's cuts to other departments despite the complaints of the other ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's advisor Eddie Goldenberg later recalled that Chrétien was unyielding in the face of efforts by other ministers to "spare" their departments, and that Chrétien kept on saying "If I change anything, everything will unravel".<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey-p266_145-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey-p266-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> In a 2011 interview, Chrétien recalled about <a href="/wiki/1995_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1995 Canadian federal budget">the 1995 budget</a> that: "There would have been a day when we would have been the Greece of today. I knew we were in a bind and we had to do something."<sup id="cite_ref-Palmer_2011_all_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Palmer_2011_all-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup> In order to silence objections from left-wing Liberal backbenchers and Cabinet ministers, Chrétien ensured that the Program Review Committee chaired by <a href="/wiki/Marcel_Mass%C3%A9" title="Marcel Massé">Marcel Massé</a> that would decide what programs to end and which to cut had a majority comprising the leftist MPs <a href="/wiki/Brian_Tobin" title="Brian Tobin">Brian Tobin</a>, Sheila Copps, <a href="/wiki/Sergio_Marchi_(politician)" title="Sergio Marchi (politician)">Sergio Marchi</a> and Herb Gray, people who would not normally be supporting cutting programs, and thereby underlined the seriousness of the crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> It was only with the budget that Martin introduced on February 27, 1995, that the Chrétien government began a policy of cuts designed to eliminate the deficit in order to reassure the markets.<sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> Much of the Liberal caucus was deeply unhappy with the 1995 budget, arguing that this was not what they had been elected for in 1993, only to be informed by the prime minister that there was no alternative.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._108_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._108-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien himself expressed his unhappiness with his budget in a radio interview with <a href="/wiki/Peter_Gzowski" title="Peter Gzowski">Peter Gzowski</a> in March 1995, saying about the budget: "It is not our pleasure sir, I have to tell you that. I've been around a long time. It's no pleasure at all. I'm not doctrinaire, a right-winger. I'm a Liberal, and I feel like a Liberal, and it is painful. But it is needed".<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._108_150-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._108-150">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government began a program of deep cuts to provincial transfers and other areas of government finance. During his tenure as prime minister, a $42&#160;billion deficit was eliminated, five consecutive budget surpluses were recorded (thanks in part to favorable economic times), $36&#160;billion in debt was paid down, and taxes were cut by $100&#160;billion (cumulatively) over five years.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CanadianEncyclopedia_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CanadianEncyclopedia-152">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup> Using the low incomes cut-offs after tax measure, the percentage of Canadians who had low income in 1993 was 14.1 percent; in 1995, when the budget was introduced, that figure had jumped to 14.5; in 2003, the end of Chrétien's time in office, that number had fallen to just 11.6 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The share of Canadians living in persistent poverty (i.e. low income for at least 3 years out of 6 years) has declined by almost half since the mid-1990s to 2010.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-153">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> Social spending as a percentage of GDP fell from 20.35 percent in 1993, to 18.35 percent in 1995, eventually falling to 16.94 percent in 1997 and 15.76 percent in 2000, and eventually rising to 16.29 percent in 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> The 1995 budget, which was called by <a href="/wiki/Peter_C._Newman" title="Peter C. Newman">Peter C. Newman</a> a "watershed document" that marked the first time in recent memory that anybody had made a serious effort to deal with the deficit, won a favorable reaction from the international markets, and a led to an immediate fall in interest rates.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> There were, however, undeniable costs associated with this endeavour. The cuts resulted in fewer government services, most noticeably in the health care sector, as major reductions in federal funding to the provinces meant significant cuts in service delivery. Moreover, the across-the-board cuts affected the operations and achievement of the mandate of most federal departments. Many of the cuts were restored in later years of Chrétien's period in office.<sup id="cite_ref-156" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-156">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In March 1996, when the Chrétien government presented <a href="/wiki/1996_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1996 Canadian federal budget">its third budget</a>, the backbencher Liberal MP John Nunziata voted against the budget under the grounds it failed to repeal the GST as the Liberals had promised in 1993 and singled out for criticism his former <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada_Rat_Pack" title="Liberal Party of Canada Rat Pack">Rat Pack</a> colleague Sheila Copps, who had promised during the 1993 election to resign within a year if the GST was not repealed.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's response was to expel Nunziata from the Liberal caucus.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> However, the expulsion of Nunziata drew attention to the fact that Copps was still in office despite her promise to resign within a year if the GST was not repealed.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien first stated that Copps would stay in Parliament despite her promise of 1993, but then intense public pressure (together with a poll showing Copps would win a by-election) forced Copps to resign from the Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> After resigning, Copps then contested the resulting by-election, where she won and then went straight back into the Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> To help defuse anger over the GST issue, in the spring of 1996 the Chrétien government moved to <a href="/wiki/Harmonized_Sales_Tax" class="mw-redirect" title="Harmonized Sales Tax">harmonize sales taxes</a> (GST with provincial taxes) by signing an accord with three of the four Atlantic provinces; the other provinces were not interested in the federal offer to harmonize.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156_157-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._156-157">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/1998_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1998 Canadian federal budget">February 1998</a>, for the first time since <a href="/wiki/1969_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1969 Canadian federal budget">1969</a> a balanced budget was presented by the government.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> Shortly afterwards, the Chrétien government introduced the National Child Benefit program for the children of low-income parents.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p204_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p204-159">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Foreign policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jesse_Flis,_Bishop_Georgije,_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg/200px-Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg/300px-Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg/400px-Jesse_Flis%2C_Bishop_Georgije%2C_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Patriarch_Pavle.jpg 2x" data-file-width="678" data-file-height="704" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien with Bishops and <a href="/wiki/Pavle,_Serbian_Patriarch" title="Pavle, Serbian Patriarch">the Patriarch</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Serbian_Orthodox_Church" title="Serbian Orthodox Church">Serbian Orthodox Church</a>, 1994.</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canada_in_the_Yugoslav_Wars">Canada in the Yugoslav Wars</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Canada in the Yugoslav Wars"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1999, Chrétien supported Canada's involvement in the <a href="/wiki/NATO" title="NATO">North Atlantic Treaty Organization</a> (NATO) <a href="/wiki/NATO_bombing_of_Yugoslavia" title="NATO bombing of Yugoslavia">bombing campaign</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Federal_Republic_of_Yugoslavia" class="mw-redirect" title="Federal Republic of Yugoslavia">Yugoslavia</a> over the issue of <a href="/wiki/Kosovo" title="Kosovo">Kosovo</a>, even through the operation was unsanctioned by the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council" title="United Nations Security Council">United Nations Security Council</a>.  There had been an Anglo-American resolution asking for the Security Council's approval of the NATO bombing, but it was vetoed by <a href="/wiki/Russia" title="Russia">Russia</a>. The idea of bombing Yugoslavia caused some discomfort within the ranks of the Liberal party as the NATO campaign effectively meant supporting Kosovo separatists against a government determined to prevent Kosovo's secession from Yugoslavia. Chrétien was personally uncomfortable with the idea of bombing Yugoslavia, but supported the war because he valued good relations with the United States far more than he cared about Yugoslavia.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's foreign minister at the time, <a href="/wiki/Lloyd_Axworthy" title="Lloyd Axworthy">Lloyd Axworthy</a> justified Canada's involvement in the bombing of Yugoslavia on the grounds that allegations of massacres against ethnic Albanians in Kosovo made the use of force legitimate on humanitarian grounds, even without the approval of the UN Security Council.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> Likewise, Chrétien was later to tell Lawrence Martin that it was far better to intervene in the internal affairs of Yugoslavia to stop human rights violations in the Kosovo region by Serbian forces than to do nothing.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217_160-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._217-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="China">China</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: China"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Chrétien was known to be a <a href="/wiki/Sinophile" title="Sinophile">Sinophile</a> and an admirer of the <a href="/wiki/China" title="China">People's Republic of China</a>. In November 1994, he led the first of four <a href="/wiki/Team_Canada_Mission" title="Team Canada Mission">"Team Canada"</a> trade missions comprising himself and nine premiers to China (Quebec Premier Jacques Parizeau having declined to go), which had as their stated objective increasing Sino-Canadian trade. The Team Canada mission was meant to be the beginning of the export offensive that would stimulate the economy out of the recession, and also to achieve Chrétien's goal going back to the 1970s of a Canadian economy less dependent on trade with the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Under his leadership, China and Canada signed several bilateral relations agreements. The Team Canada missions attracted criticism that Chrétien seemed concerned only with economic issues, that he rarely raised the subject of China's poor human rights record, and that on the few occasions that he did mention <a href="/wiki/Human_rights_in_China" title="Human rights in China">human rights in China</a> he went out of his way to avoid offending his hosts.<sup id="cite_ref-Evertt-p9-48_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Evertt-p9-48-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Moreover, Chrétien attracted criticism for presenting the case for improved human rights in purely economic terms, arguing that a better human rights record would allow China to join the WTO and thus sell more goods to the West. Chrétien argued that there was no point in criticizing China's human rights record because the Chinese never listened to such criticism, and instead were greatly annoyed about being lectured by Western leaders about their poor human rights record.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._310_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._310-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Given that Canada could not really do anything to change the views of China's leaders about human rights, Chrétien contended that the best that could be done was to improve Sino-Canadian economic relations while ignoring the subject of human rights.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._310_164-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._310-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="United_States">United States</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: United States"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg/220px-APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg/330px-APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg/440px-APEC_Summit_1993_-_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bill_Clinton_shaking_hands.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4000" data-file-height="2657" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien shaking hands with US President <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a>, at the 1993 <a href="/wiki/Asia-Pacific_Economic_Cooperation" title="Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation">APEC</a> Summit.</figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada–United States relations</a></div>
<p>Chrétien phoned U.S. President <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Bill Clinton</a> in November 1993 to ask him to renegotiate aspects of NAFTA.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Clinton bluntly refused, saying that it had been extremely difficult to get Congress to ratify NAFTA, and if NAFTA was renegotiated, then he would have to submit the renegotiated treaty again for ratification, which was not something that he was going to do just for the sake of Chrétien.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Clinton informed the prime minister that he could either scrap NAFTA or accept it as it was, and that the most he could offer were a few cosmetic concessions like writing a letter saying the United States was not interested in taking over Canada's energy and water.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79_165-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien chose the latter, and sought to portray Clinton's letter as a major American concession that constituted a renegotiated NAFTA, though in fact Clinton's letter was not legally binding and meant nothing.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79_165-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._79-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> Only treaties ratified by Congress are legally binding on the U.S. government and presidential letters impose only a moral obligation, not a legal one, on the U.S government.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the <a href="/wiki/September_11_attacks" title="September 11 attacks">September 11 attacks</a>, Canadian forces joined with multinational to pursue <a href="/wiki/Al-Qaeda" title="Al-Qaeda">al-Qaeda</a> in <a href="/wiki/Afghanistan" title="Afghanistan">Afghanistan</a>. U.S. President <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> had also commended how Canada responded to the crisis. Among them included <a href="/wiki/Operation_Yellow_Ribbon" title="Operation Yellow Ribbon">Operation Yellow Ribbon</a> and the memorial service on <a href="/wiki/Parliament_Hill" title="Parliament Hill">Parliament Hill</a> three days after 9/11. In January 2002, Chrétien together with the Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Art_Eggleton" title="Art Eggleton">Art Eggleton</a> were accused of misleading Parliament. When asked in Question Period if Canadian troops had handed over captured Taliban and al-Qaeda members in Afghanistan to the American forces amid concerns about the treatment of POWs at <a href="/wiki/Guantanamo_Bay_detention_camp" title="Guantanamo Bay detention camp">Guantanamo Bay</a>, Chrétien stated this was only a "hypothetical question" that could not be answered as the Canadians had taken no POWs.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_News_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC_News-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Critics of the government, such as Joe Clark, then pointed out that in the previous week, <i>The Globe &amp; Mail</i> had run on its front page a photo of Canadian soldiers turning over POWs to American troops.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_News_167-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC_News-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> Eggleton claimed that he had only learned of the policy of handing over POWs several days after the photo had appeared in <i>The Globe and Mail</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_News_167-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC_News-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> When pressed by opposition critics about his apparent ignorance of what was Canada's policy on turning over POWs captured in Afghanistan, Eggleton then claimed that he had not only forgotten that he had been briefed by senior bureaucrats that Canadian Forces were to hand over POWs to the Americans, but that he had also forgotten to inform the Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-Globe_and_Mail_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Globe_and_Mail-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One year after the 9/11 attacks, Chrétien gave controversial remarks about what led to the attacks, suggesting they were a reaction to Western foreign policy. During the 2002 CBC interview, Chrétien said "I do think that the Western world is getting too rich in relations to the poor world. And necessarily, we're looked upon as being arrogant, self-satisfied, greedy and with no limits. And the 11th of September is an occasion for me to realize it even more. When you are powerful like you are, you guys, it's the time to be nice. And it is one of the problems—you cannot exercise your powers to the point of humiliation of the others. And that is what the Western world—not only the Americans but the Western world—has to realize.” The comments were condemned by the new Official Opposition leader and the new Canadian Alliance leader, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a>, who charged Chretien with <a href="/wiki/Victim_blaming" title="Victim blaming">victim blaming</a>, while the leaders of the New Democratic Party and Progressive Conservative Party did not interpret Chrétien's comments as critical of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-Macleans_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Macleans-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CBC_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg/250px-Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="156" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg/375px-Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg/500px-Chr%C3%A9tien_and_Bush_shaking_hands_Sept_9_2002.jpg 2x" data-file-width="514" data-file-height="321" /></a><figcaption>President <a href="/wiki/George_W._Bush" title="George W. Bush">George W. Bush</a> and Jean Chrétien address the media before a 2002 bilateral meeting.</figcaption></figure>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Refusal_to_join_the_Iraq_War">Refusal to join the Iraq War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Refusal to join the Iraq War"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Chrétien's government did not support the US-led <a href="/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq" title="2003 invasion of Iraq">2003 invasion of Iraq</a>. His reasoning was that the war lacked UN Security Council sanction; while not a member of the Security Council, Canada nevertheless attempted to build a consensus for a resolution authorizing the use of force after a short (two- to three-month) extension to UN weapon inspections in Iraq. Critics also noted that, while in opposition, he had also opposed the first US-led <a href="/wiki/Gulf_War" title="Gulf War">Gulf War</a>, which had been approved by the UN Security Council and in 1999 supported NATO air strikes against Serbia, which had no Security Council approval. In order to avoid damaging relations with the United States, Chrétien agreed to another and larger deployment of Canadian troops to Afghanistan on February 12, 2003, in order to prove that Canada was still a good American ally, despite opposing the upcoming Iraq war.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Canada sent 2,000 soldiers to Afghanistan in the summer of 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-http_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-http-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> Twenty years later, in a French-language interview, Chretien recalled the personalities and events that led up to his refusal. At the time some in the business community were petrified that the US would look elsewhere for Canadian products; 85% of Canadian trade was with the US.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Defence_policy">Defence policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Defence policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1993, Chrétien <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Sea_King_replacement" title="Canadian Sea King replacement">canceled the contract to buy the EH-101 helicopters</a>, requiring the search for new helicopters to start over, and paid a $478&#160;million <a href="/wiki/Termination_fee" title="Termination fee">termination fee</a> to <a href="/wiki/AgustaWestland" title="AgustaWestland">AgustaWestland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1998, Chrétien's government announced that the CH-113 helicopters would be replaced by a scaled-down search-and-rescue variant of the EH101, carrying the designation <a href="/wiki/CH-149_Cormorant" class="mw-redirect" title="CH-149 Cormorant">CH-149 Cormorant</a>. Unlike the Petrel/Chimo contract which Chrétien had cancelled in 1993, these 15 aircraft were to be built entirely in Europe with no Canadian participation or industrial incentives. The first two aircraft arrived in Canada in September 2001 and entered service the following year. His Maritime Helicopter Project was supposed to find a low-cost replacement aircraft. The candidates were the <a href="/wiki/Sikorsky_S-92" title="Sikorsky S-92">Sikorsky S-92</a>, the <a href="/wiki/NHIndustries_NH90" title="NHIndustries NH90">NHIndustries NH90</a> and the EH-101, although critics accused the government of designing the project so as to prevent AgustaWestland from winning the contract. A winner, the <a href="/wiki/Sikorsky_CH-148_Cyclone" title="Sikorsky CH-148 Cyclone">Sikorsky CH-148 Cyclone</a>, would not be announced until after Chrétien retired.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-176" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reelections">Reelections</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Reelections"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="1997_federal_election">1997 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: 1997 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Chrétien called <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">an early election</a> in the spring of 1997, hoping to take advantage of his position in the public opinion polls and the continued division of the conservative vote between the Progressive Conservative Party and the upstart <a href="/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada" title="Reform Party of Canada">Reform Party of Canada</a>. Despite slipping poll numbers, he advised the governor general to call an election in 1997, a year ahead of schedule. Many of his own MPs criticized him for this move, especially in light of the devastating <a href="/wiki/Red_River_Flood,_1997" class="mw-redirect" title="Red River Flood, 1997">Red River Flood</a>, which led to charges of insensitivity. Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/John_Godfrey" title="John Godfrey">John Godfrey</a> tried hard to interest Chrétien in an ambitious plan to eliminate urban poverty in Canada as a platform to run on in the election, which was vetoed by Eddie Goldenberg and John Rae of the PMO, who convinced Chrétien that it was better to stick with an "incrementalist" course of small changes than risk any grand project.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> The Progressive Conservatives had a popular new leader in <a href="/wiki/Jean_Charest" title="Jean Charest">Jean Charest</a> and the <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="New Democratic Party (Canada)">New Democrats</a>' <a href="/wiki/Alexa_McDonough" title="Alexa McDonough">Alexa McDonough</a> led her party to a breakthrough in Atlantic Canada, where the Liberals had won all but one seat in 1993. Chrétien benefited when the Reform Party aired a TV ad in English Canada charging that the country was being dominated by French-Canadian politicians, which Chrétien used to accuse <a href="/wiki/Preston_Manning" title="Preston Manning">Preston Manning</a> of being anti-French.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> In 1997, the Liberals lost all but a handful of seats in Atlantic Canada and Western Canada, but managed to retain a bare majority government due to their continued dominance of Ontario.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="2000_federal_election">2000 federal election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: 2000 federal election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Chrétien called another <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">early election in the fall of 2000</a>, again hoping to take advantage of the split in the Canadian right and catch the newly formed <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a> and its neophyte leader <a href="/wiki/Stockwell_Day" title="Stockwell Day">Stockwell Day</a> off guard. At the funeral of Pierre Trudeau in September 2000, the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Cuba" title="President of Cuba">Cuban President</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fidel_Castro" title="Fidel Castro">Fidel Castro</a> happened to meet with Day.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p285_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p285-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Later that same day, Chrétien met with Castro, where Chrétien asked Castro about his assessment of Day and if he should call an early election or not.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p285_181-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p285-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Castro advised Chrétien to dissolve Parliament early as he considered Day to be a lightweight, and as Castro was a leader whom Chrétien respected, his advice was an important reason for the election.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p285_181-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p285-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> Finance Minister Paul Martin released a 'mini-budget' just before the election call that included significant tax cuts, a move aimed at undermining the Alliance position going into the campaign. Chrétien formed a "war room" comprising his communications director <a href="/wiki/Fran%C3%A7oise_Ducros" title="Françoise Ducros">Françoise Ducros</a>, Warren Kinsella, Duncan Fulton and Kevin Bosch to gather material to attack Day as a right-wing extremist.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup> In the first weeks of the 2000 election, the Canadian Alliance gained in the polls and some voters complained that Chrétien overstayed his time in office and had no agenda beyond staying in power for the sake of staying in power.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._292_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._292-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> The fact that the Red Book of 2000 consisted almost entirely of recycled promises from the Red Books of 1993 and 1997 and various banal statements further reinforced the impression of a prime minister with no plans or vision for Canada and whose only agenda was to hang onto power as long as possible.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> However, the Liberal claim that Day planned to dismantle the health care system to replace it with a <a href="/wiki/Two-tier_health_care" class="mw-redirect" title="Two-tier health care">"two-tier" health care system</a> along with a number of gaffes on Day's part in addition to Alliance candidate <a href="/wiki/Betty_Granger" title="Betty Granger">Betty Granger</a> warning that Canada was faced with the threat of an "Asian invasion" (which furthered the Liberals' plan to paint the Alliance as a xenophobic and extreme right-wing party)<sup id="cite_ref-Harrison,_Trevor_p._84_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Harrison,_Trevor_p._84-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> started to turn opinion decisively against the Canadian Alliance.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Day's socially conservative views were also attacked by Chrétien as the Liberals claimed that Day would make homosexuality and abortion illegal. The New Democrats and Bloc Québécois also ran lacklustre campaigns, while the Progressive Conservatives, led by former Prime Minister Joe Clark, struggled to retain official party status. On November 27, the Liberals secured a strong majority mandate in the 2000 election, winning nearly as many seats as they had in 1993, largely thanks to significant gains in Quebec and in Atlantic Canada. Without Jean Charest as leader, the PCs who had done well in winning the popular vote in Quebec in 1997 fared poorly in 2000, and most of their voters defected over to the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Scandals_and_controversies">Scandals and controversies</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Scandals and controversies"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Shawinigate">Shawinigate</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Shawinigate"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Shawinigate" title="Shawinigate">Shawinigate</a></div>
<p>In late 2000 and early 2001, politics were dominated by questions about the Grand-Mere Affair (or the <a href="/wiki/Shawinigate" title="Shawinigate">Shawinigate</a> scandal). Opposition parties frequently charged that Chrétien had broken the law in regards to his lobbying for <a href="/wiki/Business_Development_Bank_of_Canada" title="Business Development Bank of Canada">Business Development Bank of Canada</a> for loans to the Auberge Grand-Mère inn.<sup id="cite_ref-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Questions were especially centered around the firing of the president of the bank, François Beaudoin, and the involvement of Jean Carle, formerly of the PMO, in sacking Beaudoin.<sup id="cite_ref-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere_188-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Carle served as Chrétien's chief of operations between 1993 and 1998 before leaving to take up an executive post at the Business Development Bank.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien claimed that Carle was not involved in any way with the loans to the Grand-Mere Inn, only to be countered by Joe Clark, who produced a leaked document showing that he was.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> After initial denials, Chrétien acknowledged having lobbied the Business Development Bank to grant a $2&#160;million loan to Yvon Duhaime. Duhaime was a friend and constituent to whom the Prime Minister stated that he had sold his interest in the Grand-Mère Inn, a local Shawinigan-area hotel and golf resort, eventually providing evidence of the sale—a contract written on a cocktail napkin. Duhaime was a local businessman with an unsavoury reputation and a criminal record, who received a loan from the Business Development Bank that he was ineligible to collect on the account of his criminal record (Duhaime did not mention his record when applying for the loan).<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> The bank had turned down the initial loan application, but later approved a $615,000 loan following further lobbying by Chrétien. When the bank refused to extend the loan in August 1999 under the grounds that Duhaime had a bad financial history, Beaudoin was fired by Chrétien in September 1999, which led to a <a href="/wiki/Wrongful_dismissal" title="Wrongful dismissal">wrongful dismissal</a> suit that Beaudoin was to win in 2004.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup> It was revealed that Chrétien had never been paid for his share in the sale of the adjoining golf course, and criminal charges were laid against Duhaime. On February 19, 2001, the RCMP announced that there they did not find sufficient evidence to lay criminal charges against anyone in regards to the Grand-Mere Affair, and Chrétien accused Clark of waging a "witch hunt" against the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere_188-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> On March 2, 2001, the federal ethics counselor Howard Wilson cleared Chrétien of wrongdoing in the Grand-Mere Affair.<sup id="cite_ref-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere_188-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-L&#39;Affair_Grand-Mere-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> On April 5, 2001, the <i>National Post</i> received documents purportedly from an anonymous source within the bank, indicating that Chrétien was still owed $23,040 by Duhaime for his share in the Auberge Grand-Mère.<sup id="cite_ref-Cosh_2010_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Cosh_2010-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> The revelation of the Grand-Mère affair did not affect the outcome of the 2000 election.
