Chrétien and his circle believed that the breaking of the Grand-Mère story was the work of the Martin faction.<sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Sponsorship_Scandal">Sponsorship Scandal</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Sponsorship Scandal"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sponsorship_Scandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Sponsorship Scandal">Sponsorship Scandal</a></div>
<p>The major controversy of the later Chrétien years was the <a href="/wiki/Sponsorship_Scandal" class="mw-redirect" title="Sponsorship Scandal">Sponsorship Scandal</a>, which involved more than $100&#160;million distributed from the Prime Minister's Office to Quebec's federalist and Liberal Party interests without much accountability.<sup id="cite_ref-test_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-test-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup> On May 8, 2002, the Sponsorship Scandal broke when the auditor general, <a href="/wiki/Sheila_Fraser" title="Sheila Fraser">Sheila Fraser</a>, issued a report accusing Public Works bureaucrats of having broken "just about every rule in the book" in awarding $1.6&#160;million to the Montreal ad firm <a href="/wiki/Groupaction" title="Groupaction">Groupaction</a> Marketing Inc.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> The money awarded to Groupaction in three dubious contracts appeared to have disappeared, and the firm had a long history of donating to the Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358_196-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> Opposition critics further suggested that the public works minister at the time, <a href="/wiki/Alfonso_Gagliano" title="Alfonso Gagliano">Alfonso Gagliano</a>, whom Chrétien had praised as a great patriot, was not just a mere bystander to questionable contacts associated with the sponsorship program that Fraser had identified.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358_196-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> In response to the public outrage, Chrétien argued in speech in Winnipeg that all this was necessary to stop Quebec separatism and justified by the results, stating that: "Perhaps there was a few million dollars that might have been stolen in the process. It is possible. But how many millions of dollars have we saved the country because we have re-established the stability of Canada as a united country? If somebody has stolen the money, they will face the courts. But I will not apologize to Canadians."<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._359_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._359-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien's argument that he had nothing to apologize for in regards to the sponsorship program, and his apparent condoning of corruption as justified by the results of saving Canada fared poorly with the Canadian public, which increasingly started to perceive the prime minister as an autocratic leader with a thuggish streak.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._359_197-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._359-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> A poll taken later in May 2002 showed that over half of Canadians believed that the Chrétien government was corrupt.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._361_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._361-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> The Sponsorship Scandal would tarnish Chrétien's reputation only a few years after he left office, and contributed to the Liberals losing their majority government in <a href="/wiki/2004_Canadian_federal_election" title="2004 Canadian federal election">2004</a> and losing power altogether in <a href="/wiki/2006_Canadian_federal_election" title="2006 Canadian federal election">2006</a>.
</p>
<h3><span id="Chr.C3.A9tien_and_Martin:_Liberal_Party_infighting"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Chrétien_and_Martin:_Liberal_Party_infighting">Chrétien and Martin: Liberal Party infighting</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Chrétien and Martin: Liberal Party infighting"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Relations between Chrétien and Martin were frequently strained, and Martin was reportedly angling to replace Chrétien as early as 1997. Martin had long hoped that Chrétien would just retire at the end of his second term, thereby allowing him to win the Liberal leadership, and were greatly disappointed in January 2000 when Chrétien's communications director Françoise Ducros had fired "a shot across the bow" by confirming what had been strongly hinted at since the summer of 1999 in an announcement to the caucus that Chrétien would seek a third term.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien was due to face a leadership review in February 2002, but the Liberal national executive, which was controlled by partisans of Paul Martin, agreed to Chrétien's request in early January 2001 that the leadership review be pushed back to February 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p326_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p326-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> In agreeing to this request, Martin believed that this was the <i>quid pro quo</i> for allowing Chrétien a decent interval to retire with dignity sometime in 2002, an interpretation that Chrétien did not hold.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin-p326_201-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin-p326-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Rebellion_and_resignation">Rebellion and resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Rebellion and resignation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>By early 2002, the long-simmering feud with Martin came to a head. A particular concern that had badly strained relations between the prime minister and the finance minister by early 2002 was Martin's control of the Liberal Party apparatus, especially his control over the issuing of membership forms, which he reserved largely for his own supporters.<sup id="cite_ref-:6_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> In January 2002, Brian Tobin complained to Chrétien that the Liberal Party machinery had been "captured" by Martin's followers to the extent that it was now virtually impossible for anyone else to sign up their own followers.<sup id="cite_ref-:6_202-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> This posed a major problem for Chrétien as the Liberals were due to hold a leadership review in February 2003. However, it was still quite possible that Chrétien would win the review by a slim margin.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2002, an incident occurred which was to greatly damage Chrétien's relations with the Liberal caucus. After Chrétien reorganized the Cabinet in late January 2002, Liberal MP <a href="/wiki/Carolyn_Bennett" title="Carolyn Bennett">Carolyn Bennett</a> criticised Chrétien at a caucus meeting for not appointing more women to the Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien exploded with rage at Bennett's criticism, saying that as a mere backbencher she did not have the right to criticise the prime minister in front of the caucus, and attacked her with such fury that Bennett collapsed in tears.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._356_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._356-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> In February 2002, reflecting a growing number of Liberal MPs' displeasure with Chrétien, the Liberal caucus elected the outspoken pro-Martin MP <a href="/wiki/Stan_Keyes" title="Stan Keyes">Stan Keyes</a> (who had already openly mused in 2001 about how it was time for Chrétien to go) as their chairman, who defeated pro-Chrétien MP <a href="/wiki/Steve_Mahoney" title="Steve Mahoney">Steve Mahoney</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358_196-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien had expected Mahoney to win, and was reported to be shocked when he learned of Keyes's victory, which now gave Martin more control of the caucus.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358_196-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._358-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In late May 2002, Chrétien tried to curtail Martin's campaign for the leadership of the party by delivering a lecture to Cabinet to stop raising money for leadership bids within the Liberal Party. At what was described as a "stormy" Cabinet meeting on May 30, 2002, Chrétien stated that he intended to serve out his entire term, and ordered the end of all leadership fundraising.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Martin left his cabinet on June 2, 2002. Martin claimed that Chrétien dismissed him from Cabinet, while Chrétien said that Martin had resigned.<sup id="cite_ref-thestar_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thestar-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup> In his memoirs, Chrétien wrote that he regretted not having fired Martin a few years earlier.<sup id="cite_ref-thestar_207-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thestar-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Martin's departure generated a severe backlash from Martin's supporters, who controlled much of the party machinery, and all signs indicated that they were prepared to oust Chrétien at a leadership review in February 2003. To win the leadership review, Chrétien formed a team in early June 2002 comprising his close associates John Rae, <a href="/wiki/David_Collenette" title="David Collenette">David Collenette</a>, Jean Carle, and David Smith who were ordered to sign up as many pro-Chrétien ("Chrétienist") Liberals as possible for the leadership review.<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> The open split, which was covered extensively on national media, increasingly painted Chrétien as a <a href="/wiki/Lame_duck_(politics)" title="Lame duck (politics)">lame duck</a>. During the summer of 2002, a number of backbencher Liberal MPs associated with Martin started to openly criticise Chrétien's leadership, calling on him to resign now or suffer the humiliation of losing the leadership review.<sup id="cite_ref-Jeffrey_pp._375–376_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jeffrey_pp._375–376-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien asked Jim Karygiannis, who had been so effective in signing up supporters for him in 1990 to repeat that performance, only to be told by Karygiannis that Chrétien had never rewarded him by appointing him to the Cabinet as he asked for many times over the years, had not even returned his phone calls to set up a meeting to discuss his possible appointment to the Cabinet and that he was now a Martin man.<sup id="cite_ref-Martin,_Lawrence_p._383_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Martin,_Lawrence_p._383-210">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> Karygiannis then called a press conference on July 13, 2002, where he called for Chrétien to retire "with dignity", rather than risk losing a potentially divisive leadership review and avoid having his career end that way.<sup id="cite_ref-211" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-211">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After less than half the caucus committed to support him in August 2002 by signing a letter indicating their support for the prime minister in the up-coming leadership review, Chrétien announced that he would not lead the party into the next election, and set his resignation date for February 2004. Martin was not happy with the 2004 departure date, preferring that Chrétien retire at the end of 2002, but considered it better if Chrétien were to retire than having to defeat him at the 2003 leadership review, which would have been more divisive and would have established the ominous precedent of a prime minister being ousted by his own party for no other reason other that someone else wanted the job.<sup id="cite_ref-212" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-212">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup> Due to mounting pressure from the Martin camp, Chrétien no longer saw his February 2004 resignation date as tenable. His final sitting in the House of Commons took place on November 6, 2003. He made an emotional farewell to the party on November 13 at the <a href="/wiki/2003_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="2003 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">2003 Liberal leadership convention</a>. The following day, Martin was elected his successor. On December 12, 2003, Chrétien formally resigned as prime minister, handing power over to Martin. Chrétien joined the law firm, <a href="/wiki/Heenan_Blaikie" title="Heenan Blaikie">Heenan Blaikie</a> on January 5, 2004, as counsel. The firm announced he would work out of its Ottawa offices four days per week and make a weekly visit to the Montreal office. In early 2004, there occurred much <a href="/wiki/2004_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_infighting" class="mw-redirect" title="2004 Liberal Party of Canada infighting">in-fighting</a> within the Liberal Party with several Liberal MPs associated with Chrétien such as Sheila Copps and <a href="/wiki/Charles_Caccia" title="Charles Caccia">Charles Caccia</a> losing their nomination battles against Martin loyalists.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Retirement"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg/220px-Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg/330px-Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg/440px-Liberal_rally_Brampton_2008_election_82.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2576" data-file-height="1920" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/St%C3%A9phane_Dion" title="Stéphane Dion">Stéphane Dion</a> makes a speech on October 10, 2008, in <a href="/wiki/Brampton_West_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Brampton West (federal electoral district)">Brampton West</a>. Former Prime Minister Jean Chrétien was among notable Liberals at this rally; this was his first time campaigning for anyone since retirement.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On February 18, 2004, François Beaudoin won his wrongful dismissal suit against the Business Development Bank of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-National_Post_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Post-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> Justice Andre Denis ruled in favor of Beaudoin's claim that he was fired for political reasons in 1999 for trying to call the loan on the Grand Mère Inn, ruled that Chrétien's former aide Jean Carle and <a href="/wiki/Michel_Vennat" title="Michel Vennat">Michel Vennat</a> was guilty of making false criminal and civil charges of wrongdoing against Beaudoin to discredit him for suing the bank, accused Carle of committing perjury during the trial and declared given the "unspeakable injustice" Beaudoin had suffered, told the government not to appeal his ruling because they would be wasting the tax-payers' money if they did.<sup id="cite_ref-National_Post_213-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-National_Post-213">&#91;213&#93;</a></sup> The lingering repercussions of the sponsorship scandal of 2002 reduced the Liberal Party to a minority in the 2004 election, may have strengthened the separatist case, and contributed to the government's defeat in the 2006 election. The scandal led to long-running, deep investigations by the RCMP, a federal inquiry, the <a href="/wiki/Gomery_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Gomery Commission">Gomery Commission</a>, chaired by Justice <a href="/wiki/John_Gomery" title="John Gomery">John Gomery</a> (called by Martin in 2004), and several prosecutions and convictions; the legal process continued to late 2011, more than a decade after the scandal began.
</p><p>Jean Chrétien testified for the Gomery Commission regarding the sponsorship scandal in February 2005. Earlier that year his lawyers tried, but failed, to have Justice John Gomery removed from the commission, arguing that he lacked objectivity.<sup id="cite_ref-214" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-214">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien contends that the Gomery Commission was set up to tarnish his image, and that it was not a fair investigation. He cites comments Gomery made calling him "small town cheap", referring to the management of the sponsorship program as "catastrophically bad", and calling <a href="/wiki/Chuck_Guit%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Chuck Guité">Chuck Guité</a> a "charming scamp". Subsequent to the release of the first report, Chrétien has decided to take an action in <a href="/wiki/Federal_Court_of_Canada" title="Federal Court of Canada">Federal Court</a> to review the commission report on the grounds that Gomery showed a "reasonable apprehension of bias", and that some conclusions didn't have an "evidentiary" basis.<sup id="cite_ref-215" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-215">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> Chrétien believes that the appointment of <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Roy" title="Bernard Roy">Bernard Roy</a>, a former chief of staff to former PC prime minister Brian Mulroney, as chief counsel for the commission was a mistake, as he failed to call some relevant witnesses such as <a href="/wiki/Don_Boudria" title="Don Boudria">Don Boudria</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Goodale" title="Ralph Goodale">Ralph Goodale</a>. In his report of November 1, 2005, on responsibility for the sponsorship scandal, Justice Gomery ruled that Chrétien was not responsible for the awarding of advertising contracts in Quebec in which millions were stolen, but did accept <a href="/wiki/Charles_Guit%C3%A9" title="Charles Guité">Charles Guité</a>'s claim that he received his instructions on what program to sponsor and to spend how much money on each program from <a href="/wiki/Jean_Pelletier" title="Jean Pelletier">Jean Pelletier</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Chief_of_Staff_to_the_Prime_Minister_(Canada)" title="Chief of Staff to the Prime Minister (Canada)">chief of staff</a> at the PMO between 1993 and 2001 and Jean Carle, the director of operations at the PMO between 1993 and 1998 as the truth.<sup id="cite_ref-216" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-216">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_(5663165931).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg/220px-Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="136" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg/330px-Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg/440px-Ignatieff_and_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_Rise_Up_for_Canada_Rally_013_%285663165931%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5562" data-file-height="3438" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien at the Rise Up For Canada rally, 2011</figcaption></figure>
<p>In April 2007, Chrétien and Canadian book publishers <a href="/wiki/Knopf_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Knopf Canada">Knopf Canada</a> and Éditions du Boréal announced they would be publishing his memoirs, <i>My Years as Prime Minister</i>, which would recount Chrétien's tenure as prime minister. The book was announced under the title of <i>A Passion for Politics</i>. It arrived in bookstores in October 2007, in both English and French, but the promotional tour was delayed due to heart surgery. As well <i>Straight from the Heart</i> was republished with a new preface and two additional chapters detailing his return to politics as the leader of the Liberal Party and his victory in the election of 1993. Publisher <a href="/wiki/Key_Porter_Books" title="Key Porter Books">Key Porter Books</a> timed the re-issuing to coincide with the publication of <i>My Years as Prime Minister</i>.
</p><p>On October 1, 2007, Chrétien was playing at the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Montreal_Golf_Club" title="Royal Montreal Golf Club">Royal Montreal Golf Club</a>, north of Montreal, at a charity golf event. Playing alongside a <a href="/wiki/Cardiologist" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardiologist">cardiologist</a>, he mentioned his discomfort, saying he "had been suffering some symptoms for some time" and the doctor advised he come for a check up. After examination, Chrétien was hospitalized at the <a href="/wiki/Montreal_Heart_Institute" title="Montreal Heart Institute">Montreal Heart Institute</a>, with <a href="/wiki/Unstable_angina" title="Unstable angina">unstable angina</a>, a sign a heart attack might be imminent. He underwent <a href="/wiki/Quadruple_heart_bypass_surgery" class="mw-redirect" title="Quadruple heart bypass surgery">quadruple heart bypass surgery</a> as a result on the morning of October 3, 2007. The operation forced Chrétien to delay a promotional tour for his book. He was "expected to have a full and complete recovery".<sup id="cite_ref-217" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-217">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 2008, Chrétien and former NDP leader <a href="/wiki/Ed_Broadbent" title="Ed Broadbent">Ed Broadbent</a> came out of retirement to negotiate a formal coalition agreement between the Liberals, the New Democratic Party and the Bloc Québécois, the first power-sharing coalition since the Union government of 1917–1918 founded in response to the conscription crisis caused by <a href="/wiki/World_War_I" title="World War I">World War I</a>, in a bid to form a new government to replace the government of Prime Minister Stephen Harper. Harper's request to prorogue parliament was granted by Governor General <a href="/wiki/Micha%C3%ABlle_Jean" title="Michaëlle Jean">Michaëlle Jean</a>, staving off the opposition's scheduled motion of non-confidence.<sup id="cite_ref-218" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-218">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On August 5, 2010, Chrétien complained of experiencing difficulty walking, and was admitted to a hospital.<sup id="cite_ref-natpost_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-natpost-219">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-brainsurgery_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brainsurgery-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> A brain scan was conducted the next day, and it revealed that a 3 centimeter wide <a href="/wiki/Subdural_hematoma" title="Subdural hematoma">subdural hematoma</a> was pushing 1.5 centimeters into his brain. Emergency surgery was then performed that afternoon, and the blood was successfully drained.<sup id="cite_ref-brainsurgery_220-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-brainsurgery-220">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> He was released from hospital on August 9, 2010. Doctors, who were impressed with the speed of his recovery, ordered him to rest for two to four weeks.<sup id="cite_ref-221" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-221">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien's name was rumoured as a replacement for <a href="/wiki/Kofi_Annan" title="Kofi Annan">Kofi Annan</a> as <a href="/wiki/Secretary-General_of_the_United_Nations" title="Secretary-General of the United Nations">secretary-general of the United Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-222" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-222">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien is a member of the <a href="/wiki/Jacques_Chirac_Foundation_for_Sustainable_Development_and_Cultural_Dialogue" class="mw-redirect" title="Jacques Chirac Foundation for Sustainable Development and Cultural Dialogue">Fondation Chirac</a>'s honour committee,<sup id="cite_ref-223" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-223">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup> ever since the foundation was launched in 2008 by former French president Jacques Chirac in order to promote world peace. He is also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Club_de_Madrid" class="mw-redirect" title="Club de Madrid">Club de Madrid</a>, a group of former leaders from democratic countries, that works to strengthen democracy and respond to global crises.<sup id="cite_ref-224" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-224">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup> Jean Chrétien is also an Honorary Member of The International Raoul Wallenberg Foundation.<sup id="cite_ref-225" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-225">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien is named in the <a href="/wiki/Paradise_Papers" title="Paradise Papers">Paradise Papers</a>, a set of confidential electronic documents relating to <a href="/wiki/Offshore_investment" title="Offshore investment">offshore investment</a> that were leaked to the German newspaper <i><a href="/wiki/S%C3%BCddeutsche_Zeitung" title="Süddeutsche Zeitung">Süddeutsche Zeitung</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-226" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-226">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chrétien was appointed a Companion of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a> on June 29, 2007.<sup id="cite_ref-227" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-227">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-228" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-228">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> He was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Merit" title="Order of Merit">Order of Merit</a> by Queen <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II" title="Elizabeth II">Elizabeth II</a> in July 2009<sup id="cite_ref-Chrétien_grateful_for_honour_from_Q_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Chrétien_grateful_for_honour_from_Q-229">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> and received the insignia of the order from the Queen on October 20, 2009.<sup id="cite_ref-230" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-230">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg/220px-Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="141" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg/330px-Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg/440px-Justin_Trudeau_and_Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_at_campaign_rally_in_Hamilton.jpg 2x" data-file-width="897" data-file-height="575" /></a><figcaption>Chrétien at a rally in support of <a href="/wiki/Justin_Trudeau" title="Justin Trudeau">Justin Trudeau</a>, 2015</figcaption></figure>
<p>In December 2011, Chrétien claimed that the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party</a> and their majority government would overturn the legalization of same-sex marriage and abortion.<sup id="cite_ref-231" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-231">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup> In March 2013, Chrétien criticized Stephen Harper's foreign policy, sparking some debate about the different degrees of influence Canada has held in foreign affairs under the two prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-232" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-232">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> On September 12, 2015, Chrétien published an open letter to Canadian voters in multiple newspapers in which he criticized Harper's response to the <a href="/wiki/European_migrant_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="European migrant crisis">European migrant crisis</a>, stating that Harper has turned Canada into a "cold hearted" nation and he has "shamed Canada". "I am sad to see that in fewer than 10 years, the Harper government has tarnished almost 60 years of Canada's reputation as a builder of peace and progress.", Chrétien stated before imploring voters to topple the Harper government in <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">the upcoming election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-233" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-233">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2021, Chrétien faced controversy during a promotional press interview for his recent book publication in which he denied having knowledge of the ongoing abuse happening to indigenous children in residential schools during his time as Indian Affairs Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-234" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-234">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> He went on to compare the experience of Indian Residential Schools, which inflicted lifelong psychological and physical trauma and led either directly or indirectly to the deaths of thousands of children who attended the schools, to his own experience at a private boarding school. NDP MP <a href="/wiki/Charlie_Angus" title="Charlie Angus">Charlie Angus</a> contested that the residential school abuse was reported to the department while Chrétien was minister, citing a letter from a teacher at St. Anne's Residential School in Fort Albany, Ont. It was dated 1968. In it, the teacher told him “that crimes are being committed against children," and "that he as Indian affairs minister had to step up and do something." Chrétien never responded.<sup id="cite_ref-235" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-235">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In September 2022, Chrétien attended Elizabeth II's <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Elizabeth_II" title="Death and state funeral of Elizabeth II">state funeral</a>, along with other former Canadian prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-236" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-236">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Legacy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_(5034822983).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg/250px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg/375px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/85/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg/500px-Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien_wax_figure_-_Royal_London_Wax_Museum_%285034822983%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3454" data-file-height="2302" /></a><figcaption>Wax figure of Chrétien at the Royal London Wax Museum</figcaption></figure>
<p>Chrétien was ranked the 9th greatest prime minister in a survey of Canadian scholars in 1999, which appeared in <i>Prime Ministers: Ranking Canada's Leaders</i> by <a href="/wiki/J.L._Granatstein" class="mw-redirect" title="J.L. Granatstein">J.L. Granatstein</a> and <a href="/wiki/Norman_Hillmer" title="Norman Hillmer">Norman Hillmer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-237" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-237">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup> <i><a href="/wiki/Maclean%27s" title="Maclean&#39;s">Maclean's</a></i> has consistently ranked Chrétien in the top ten on their assessments of Canadian prime ministers; he was ranked 9th greatest in 1997, 6th greatest in 2011 and 7th greatest in 2016.
</p><p>Writing in <i>Policy Options</i>, historian and author <a href="/wiki/Bob_Plamondon" title="Bob Plamondon">Bob Plamondon</a> pointed out that "After demonizing Chrétien, Quebec nationalists could not reconcile themselves to the reality that he gave their province new tools to protect the French language and culture. Canada had not been as united in the previous 50 years as when Chrétien left office. Economists were left to wonder how Chrétien turned around the national finances without triggering a recession. Canadians said in a survey that staying out of Iraq was the country's greatest foreign policy achievement. University presidents still marvel at how Chrétien rebuilt Canada's intellectual infrastructure, turning a brain drain into a brain gain."<sup id="cite_ref-238" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-238">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historian <a href="/wiki/Michael_Bliss" title="Michael Bliss">Michael Bliss</a> wrote that Chrétien was "moderately competent and only moderately corrupt."<sup id="cite_ref-239" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-239">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> Bliss also wrote, "Jean Chrétien's career shows how much can be accomplished in Canadian politics by someone who is ambitious, hard-working, and has good luck — even if they speak English with a very thick accent."<sup id="cite_ref-240" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-240">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Supreme_Court_appointments">Supreme Court appointments</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Supreme Court appointments"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Chrétien chose the following jurists to be appointed as justices of the Supreme Court of Canada:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Michel_Bastarache" title="Michel Bastarache">Michel Bastarache</a> (September 30, 1997&#160;– June 30, 2008)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/William_Ian_Corneil_Binnie" class="mw-redirect" title="William Ian Corneil Binnie">William Ian Corneil Binnie</a> (January 8, 1998&#160;– October 21, 2011)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Louise_Arbour" title="Louise Arbour">Louise Arbour</a> (September 15, 1999&#160;– June 30, 2004)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Louis_LeBel" title="Louis LeBel">Louis LeBel</a> (January 7, 2000 – November 30, 2014)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Beverly_McLachlin" class="mw-redirect" title="Beverly McLachlin">Beverly McLachlin</a> (as chief justice, July 7, 2000 – December 15, 2017; appointed a <a href="/wiki/Puisne_Justice" class="mw-redirect" title="Puisne Justice">puisne justice</a> under Prime Minister Mulroney, March 30, 1989)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Marie_Deschamps" title="Marie Deschamps">Marie Deschamps</a> (August 7, 2002 – August 7, 2012)</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Morris_J._Fish" class="mw-redirect" title="Morris J. Fish">Morris J. Fish</a> (August 5, 2003 – August 31, 2013)</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Honours"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="center" style="width:auto; margin-left:auto; margin-right:auto;">

<table class="wikitable mw-collapsible" style="max-width: 100%">
<caption class="nowrap">Coat of arms of Jean Chrétien
</caption>
<tbody><tr>
<td style="width: 220px;"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png/220px-Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="242" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png/330px-Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/81/Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png/440px-Chr%C3%A9tien_Escutcheon.png 2x" data-file-width="852" data-file-height="938" /></a></span>
</td>
<td style="min-width:175px;">
<dl><dt>Crest</dt>
<dd>Issuant from flames Or a phoenix wings elevated and addorsed Azure beaked and crested Gules holding in its beak an open scroll proper;</dd>
<dt>Escutcheon</dt>
<dd>Gules a beehive Or with three bees Argent embellished Sable, in the canton the mark of the Prime Ministership of Canada (four maple leaves conjoined in cross) Argent;</dd>
<dt>Supporters</dt>
<dd>Two polar bears proper each charged on the shoulder with a Latin cross pendent from each crossbeam two balance pans Gules, standing on a rocky mount proper set with maple leaves Gules and fleurs-de-lis Azure and issuant from barry-wavy Argent and Azure;</dd>
<dt>Motto</dt>
<dd>LABORARE AD AEDIFICANDUM (Work To Build)<sup id="cite_ref-247" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-247">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
</td></tr></tbody></table>
